
===== [p47-47] FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Explanation of picture: (3块) =====
--[p47]-- The focus range gives us some margin for error and is called the depth of focus (DOF).
There is a similar system to put and maintain the reticle in the focal plane above the lens, but this system is part of the Scanning Reticle Stage subsystem.
--[p47]-- During calibration, the Level Sensor is told where to assume the focal plane is.
If a deviation of the wafer surface from this calibrated plane is measured, actuators in the wafer stage will immediately react to put the wafer surface back in the focal plane and keep it there.
Note that in general there is a vertical range in which the projected image is - for all features to be transferred - sufficiently sharp. The focus range gives us some margin for error and is called the depth of focus (DOF).
--[p47]-- Exposing a wafer involves transfer of an image on a reticle above the projection lens into the photoactive coating on the wafer. If we expose ‘out of focus’, e.g. with the wafer surface outside the focal plane, then we will not get a proper image transfer.
The Level Sensor measures height and orientation of the wafer surface. It does this at the x,y position of the focal plane, I.e. underneath the lens. During calibration, the Level Sensor is told where to assume the focal plane is.

--[p266]-- This slide shows how using multiple orders reduces the process sensitivity:
• first order only measures centre of gravity
• higher orders carry information about the edges of the lines, which are generally undistorted
This is also shown on the right: when the higher Fourier components are added the ‘image’ looks more like the original block
Note: ATHENA will NOT add the different signals! Every signal is detected separately and can be used separately.

===== [p96-98] ADVANCED RETICLE MANAGEMENT SYSTEM (ARMS) > Parameters and Offsets (13块) =====
--[p97]-- Its function is to prevent dust particles from reaching the fragile structures on the reticle.
If a dust particle sticks to the pellicle, it is out of focus and it will not disturb the imaging of the reticle on the wafer. The pellicle is quite fragile, and therefore it has the nice side effect that a reticle is treated with the required care (at least, in most cases it is). A damaged pellicle is a visible sign that the reticle has been mistreated.
DUV pellicles are made of polyethylene, while those for longer wavelength illumination may be made of nitrocellulose.
--[p96]-- The load positions offsets can be found in:
There’s one set of offsets for each gripper, specifying the difference between the reticle position achieved by pre-aligned and the “best” reticle position measured by the alignment system. The difference between these two positions is caused by manufacturing tolerance of the LED/quadcell position and reticle movement after pre-alignment (during actual loading). This MC will change the reticle position by moving the gripper in X, Y and Rz to correct for this error.
--[p98]-- Note: during the whole sequence the plunger is closed.
The time it takes to retrieve, inspect and put back the reticle is typically the exposure time for 2 or 3 wafers and can easily be included in a batch. If IRIS works within batch streaming, it takes no (or very little) production time.
--[p96]-- To guarantee correct loading of the reticle with the required accuracy for alignment (± 3 µm), several parameters are relevant.
 Gripper open/close time
 R-pin up/down movement is fixed
 Load position offsets (X, Y, Rz)
 Pre-align above chuck correction (X, Y, Rz)
 Mirror to reticle rotation (Rotation)
ARMS 48142 v00, 13/04/00 10
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
During movement the reticle is clamped in the gripper with a plunger (gripper closed, means reticle is clamped).
--[p98]-- The reticle is scanned for particles, scanning in Y is done by moving the gripper, scanning in X is done by moving the laser beam
After the scan, the results are calculated and shown on the operator console (in graphical and table format) Depending on the settings and outcome of the scan, the reticle is loaded onto the scanning reticle stage or put back in the carrier
Note: during the whole sequence the plunger is closed.
--[p97]-- The pellicle will make sure that a particle is out of focus.
In the past two pellicles could be applied, one for the chrome side (bottom) and one for the glass side (top). However since reticles have become thicker, pellicles on top of reticles are not needed any more.
A pellicle is a thin film that is attached to the pellicle frame or cover plate. Its function is to prevent dust particles from reaching the fragile structures on the reticle.
--[p96]-- This MC will correct for any reticle to scanning reticle stage mirror rotation.
This MC is determined during an XY test. The correction is applied by rotating the scanning reticle stage.
The result of all these corrections is a reticle pre-alignment accuracy of about ±3 µm.
If there are particles on the reticle the light path is disturbed and the image can have a ’flaw’. This error will be visible on all exposure fields. Depending on size and number of the particles a reticle can still be used or should be cleaned.
--[p97]-- Depending on size and number of the particles a reticle can still be used or should be cleaned.
The influence of particles is decreased by bringing these particles out of focus. We achieve that in the following ways:
•The chrome pattern is on the bottom side of the reticle, so if a particle falls down on top of the reticle it is out of focus. To enhance this effect and to increase mechanical strength, reticles have become thicker.
•By putting pellicles on the reticle. The pellicle will make sure that a particle is out of focus.
--[p98]-- The purpose of IRIS is to scan the reticle for the presence of particles and give an advise to the operator to use or reject the reticle. Systems with release 8.2 (or higher) can be equipped with an Integrated Reticle Inspection System (IRIS).
The hardware difference for an IRIS system is that a particle detector (the IRIS unit) replaces one of the libraries (port 2 or 3). This means that a maximum of 12 reticles are available.
Sequence of events:
--[p96]-- The time needed to open or close the plunger is measured during a test. If the plunger closes too quick, it can put tension on the gripper arms, because it ‘pushes too hard’. When the reticle is released above the reticle stage for fine pre-alignment, the tension is taken off and the reticle could move due to vibration. This will destroy the coarse to fine pre-alignment accuracy. The open/close time is measured with the aid of two vso’s (vane switch oscillator). A vso is a proximity detector.
--[p96]-- The pre-align above chuck correction can be found in:
Machine constants / RS: Reticle Stage / Exchange / Prealign Above Chuck Correction X, Y, Rz
Currently this MC is not used. Maybe in future it will be used to correct the remaining loading error.
The mirror to reticle rotation can be found in:
Machine constants / RS: Reticle Stage / Exchange / Mirror to reticle rotation / Rotation
This MC will correct for any reticle to scanning reticle stage mirror rotation.
--[p96]-- A vso is a proximity detector.
The specifications for gripper open and close time are: close 250 ± 100ms, open 250 ± 100 ms.
R-pins up/down speed is fixed to 60 mm/s. This is fast enough to achieve a high throughput and slow enough to guarantee the reticle isn’t jarred while moving to the reticle stage.
The load positions offsets can be found in:
Machine constants / RH: Reticle Handling / Load Position Constants / Upper (Lower) Robot / Load Position Offset X, Y, Rz
--[p98]-- The gripper takes a reticle from a library (same as loading sequence explained on slide 3)
The reticle is coarse pre-aligned
The gripper moves to the particle detector position (port 2 or 3)
The gripper moves in Z to focus the top side or bottom side of the reticle with respect to the particle detector The gripper inserts the reticle into the particle detector
The laser of the particle detector is switched on

===== [p138-139] Stage Movement Design > Linear motor (detail) (1 of 2) (7块) =====
--[p139]-- \- We have a three to two ratio of magnets (three top magnets take up the same space as two bottom magnets).
\- The voltages on every three coils must always be 1/3 (phase shifted 120- degrees) out of phase with each other. (So all coils with the same label (1, 2 or 3) are in phase. To move from left to right as in the picture, Coil 1 is 1/3 behind in phase with respect to coil 2 and coil 2 is 1/3 behind in phase with respect to coil 3.)
--[p138]-- Two bodies, separated by air are present in the ASML design.
Both bodies have arrays of magnets attached to them. One body has permanent magnets, the other has electromagnets attached to its underside. Electromagnets are required to modulate field strength on one side of the motor (either “rotor” or “stator”).
The coil body floats on the other body. An air cushion maintains the distance between both bodies. It absorbs the vertical component of the forces exerted by the magnets.
--[p138]-- It absorbs the vertical component of the forces exerted by the magnets.
The illustration shows that all permanent magnets are oriented in one direction, with North facing the observer. The strength and direction of the electromagnets’ fields are tuned individually by varying the voltage applied to each coil. In this illustration, a capital letter stands for a strong pole (large voltage applied to coil), a lower case letter means the electromagnet is less strong. Notice the repetition of the magnet strengths for every three electromagnets.
--[p138]-- Notice the repetition of the magnet strengths for every three electromagnets.
If the electromagnets are energized as shown in the illustration, the top body (rotor) is stationary. If the top body is moved slightly to the right or left, the resulting force imbalance will drive it back to its original position. So, unless the voltage to the magnets is changed, the top body will not move!
To induce movement, the voltages on the coils are varied according to the sine functions as seen in the graph. The electromagnetic strength is linearly dependent on these voltages.
--[p139]-- h the same label (1, 2 or 3) are in phase.
In summary, the linear motor can provide us the large movement range and high acceleration that we need. Combined with a Lorentz motor, we also achieve the required precision.
--[p138]--  Working principle:
– Opposite magnetic poles attract,
– Equal magnetic poles repel.
– Use permanent magnets and coils to induce magnetic fields and control the current through the coils to control top body position.
The picture displays the global idea of the linear motor. The purpose of the motor is to follow a set dynamic path (at a set speed and with a set acceleration) from initial to final position.
Two bodies, separated by air are present in the ASML design.
--[p139]-- The electromagnetic strength is linearly dependent on these voltages.
The coils will arrange themselves on the permanent magnets, to conform to the three magnetic strengths. The movement speed depends on how fast the voltages on the coils are changed. (Meaning the frequency of the control signal: the amplitude of the control signal is always maximum.) The movement direction depends on whether the control signal follows the graph from left to right, or from right to left.
Important for the system to work is that:

--[p315]-- It is the width of this frequency range, the peak width, that is important.
Newer generation machines are using lasers as a light source with lower wavelengths. The trend in the past has been and in the future will be that the exposure wavelength will decrease further. There are several advantages to using a laser.
\- Lower wavelength with still high intensity
\- Very narrow peak width, reducing some imaging errors
\- Control over the wavelength
--[p321]-- If a sudden loss of illumination is observed on a DUV system, and the laser operating parameters seem stable, it is not uncommon to find a laser delivery tube which has been kicked or bumped, causing it to be dented or crimped. Often the laser is located remotely from the lithography system, and opportunity for such damage is greater in a service chase where tool carts and chemical delivery is routine.

--[p316]-- The DUV laser provides the light used for exposure.
The 248nm DUV laser is a Krypton/Fluorine laser. The 193nm DUV laser is an Argon/Fluorine laser. The laser has a high voltage control system to control the pulse intensity up to 10MW. There is also a beam steering and shaping system on the output of the laser, as well as a wavelength control system.
This slide shows diagrammatically the laser and beam delivery part of the optical chain for the PAS 5500/500. Main features and benefits of the laser and beam delivery system are:
--[p318]-- Cymer ELS 5600 laser, operating at 1000 Hz, giving 10 Watt maximum continuous output power; pulse energy under scanner control (based on feedback signal from Energy Sensor in AERIAL illuminator).
<= 0.8 pm FWHM (= Full Width at Half Maximum) bandwidth (spectral purity)
\+ 0.25 pm wavelength stability; built-in absolute wavelength calibration; feed forward wavelength correction to compensate focus drifts induced by barometric pressure changes.
--[p326]-- This is done to protect the optics from premature failure, because some absorption of energy occurs in the optics, and since a small sharp point laser beam hitting optical surfaces will cause more damage, more quickly, than a wide dispersed array of light.
Pupil shaping optics
--[p324]-- The slide displays a picture of the bottom module.

The bottom module consists of (in the direction of the exposure light):
– beam steering unit; Adjusts position and direction of the exposure light as it is directed up towards the various optics.
– variable attenuator; Attenuates the pulse energy. Depending on the orientation of the attenuator, a percentage of the pulse will be transmitted, while the remainder of its energy is directed towards the beam dump.
--[p315]-- \- Very narrow peak width, reducing some imaging errors
The disadvantage of a laser is that in general the intensity on wafer level is much lower(2900 mW/cm 2 for the /400 versus 900 mW/cm 2 for the /700). This means that with the same resist type and therefore the same exposure dose, the exposure time would be longer. This would have a negative impact on throughput. Therefore on DUV systems a special type of resist is used, called chemically amplified photoresist.
--[p324]-- – DOE 2; Diffractive Optical Element 2; DOE 2 shapes the light that exits the pupil shaping optics to fit the quart rod dimensions. After DOE 2, the exposure light exits the bottom module and enters the quartz rod in the top module.
The variable attenuator controls dose accuracy at wafer level, and is fully controlled by the IL software and the dose control software. The variable attenuator can reduce the laser beam intensity to allow transmission of very small doses.

--[p315]-- These will be discussed in this order on the subsequent slides.
A /400 uses a high pressure mercury arc lamp as a light source. From the emission spectrum of a mercury lamp we use the peak labeled ‘i’. All light except the light from this i-line is filtered out, so that we have a nice, ‘single wavelength’ illumination. Single wavelength? Well... not really. In the i-line peak we have a range of frequencies. It is the width of this frequency range, the peak width, that is important.

--[p325]-- The variable attenuator is made of a dielectric coating on a quartz plate. The variable attenuator reduces the intensity (energy) of the laser beam by tilting the quartz plate.
Transmission depends on the angle of the coated plate with respect to the incoming laser beam. The angle is selected using the IL software. The nominal angle is 45, where approximately 50% of the light is transmitted. For transmissions larger than 98%, the angle is 40, and for 10% transmission, the angle is 50.
--[p322]-- AERIAL stands for Automatic Enhancement Resolution Imaging for Advanced Lithography.
The AERIAL Illuminator consists of the Bottom Module and a Top Module. The bottom module contains a beam steering unit, illumination shaping optics, and an attenuation system. The top module contains a quartz rod, the energy sensor, the REMA blades, and the REMA lens.
The purpose of the illuminator is to create a field of light at reticle level that is highly uniform and has coherence properties as created/modified in the pupil shaping optics.
--[p315]-- Therefore on DUV systems a special type of resist is used, called chemically amplified photoresist.
These kind of photoresist require less exposure dose and so the throughput on DUV systems can be very high. The disadvantage of the chemically amplified resists is that these are very sensitive to contamination and delay (time between coating, exposure and development).
Typical exposure doses for I-line resist: 50-300 mJ/cm 2 . Typical exposure doses for DUV resist: 2-30 mJ/cm 2 .

--[p326]-- In a laser illuminated system, DOE 1 diffracts laser light to disperse the beam and to start the process of illumination field shaping and uniformity optimisation.
DOE 1 consists of lenses that diffuse the laser light without absorption. The lenses have specially shaped grooves to give the laser light a scatter angle of 5.
DOE 1 is responsible for taking a highly collimated laser beam and diffracting it out to a larger diameter beam to be used in the pupil shaping optics.
--[p320]-- The tubes are purged with nitrogen in order to minimize contamination of the mirrors.
During the installation, the mirrors are positioned optimally. However, the machines might drift a bit in position and the mirrors in beam delivery might drift a bit to. To keep any drift from influencing the path of the exposure light through the exposure tool there is a beam steering unit. The beam steering unit automatically adjusts position and orientation of the beam.

===== [p193-193] LEVEL SENSOR (LS) FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Scanner Routing Possibilities (3块) =====
--[p193]-- So a lot of extra stage movement is required, lowering the throughput.
To enhance throughput, an optimized route can be calculated. This means that the scanner will not expose the standard route, but a route that can be exposed faster.
--[p193]-- The normal route is the same as the route for steppers.
This means that first the inner fields for tilt are exposed in a meander and after that intra and inter fields for tilt. This has a big negative impact on throughput since a lot of fields have a fixed scan direction due to level sensor restrictions. It means that the scanner will expose in positive Y-direction, than move back and then expose the next die in positive Y-direction again. So a lot of extra stage movement is required, lowering the throughput.
--[p193]--  Two possibilities:
\- normal route -optimized route
 Scanner route can be similar to stepper route (normal route = meander)
Penalty is low throughput because for a lot of fields the exposure direction is fixed due to level sensor functionality
To optimize throughput an optimized route can be calculated
 This will take into account the level sensor functionality and minimize the time needed to expose a batch

===== [p341-341] Introduction to semiconductor industry FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Lithography printing types: (3块) =====
--[p341]-- If the mask would actually touch the wafer, the technique is called contact printing. Contact printing actually preceded proximity printing. Obviously, the drawback of this method is that both wafer and mask are easily damaged. Proximity printing is a compromise between this damage and the half shadow effect.
If a projection lens is used to project the mask pattern onto the wafer, the technique is called projection printing.
--[p341]-- Major disadvantage of this technique is that you need a very good projection lens and have only a limited depth of focus at both the mask and the wafer plane. Advantage is that both mask and wafer remain undamaged, and that you can build in a reduction factor from mask to wafer. This is how ASML machines work. In general, for small features, it is the preferred method.
--[p341]-- The left picture represents the exposure step. Displayed is a technique where the mask is close to the wafer, but not touching. This is called proximity printing. Issues with proximity printing are that half-shadow effects will be created if not all the light is directed perpendicular to the mask. (half-shadow effect: no sharp transition between exposed and not exposed area) Notice that the printed pattern is the same size as the mask pattern.

===== [p482-483] Dose Control FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY >  Sloped slit intensity profile: (6块) =====
--[p483]--  Scanner measures pulse energy (in AERIAL illuminator)
 Generates energy set point to laser for next pulse (on the fly)
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
Dose control
Because we use pulsed laser, pulses must be synchronized to scan speed -”full bucket model” from intro
Pulse energy also needs accurate control
/300 uses “laser dose control” - laser controls dose energy per pulse.
Scanner needs higher accuracy, so new mode - “scanner dose control”
\- calculates moving average i.e. average during scan
--[p482]-- Slit intensity profile
Single pulse dose build-up
Multiple pulses dose build-up
Effective slit width
Intensity
Position in slit Dose
Position on die
Position on wafer
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
On a scanner a slit is used for exposing wafers.
The intensity distribution in the slit has sloped sidewalls (lower left graph) instead of straight sidewalls (top left graph)
This decreases the effect that stage synchronization errors have on dose uniformity. (compare top middle graph to bottom middle graph)
--[p482]-- This decreases the effect that stage synchronization errors have on dose uniformity.
To further reduce the effect of stage synchronization errors on dose uniformity, the dose is build-up of multiple laser pulses. (compare top right graph to bottom right graph)
For static exposures of marks, the middle part of the slit intensity profile is kept uniform. To indicate the width of a sloped slit intensity profile, the effective slit width is
introduced. It is measured at half the maximum intensity of the slit intensity profile. This width may differ between systems.
--[p483]-- Scanner needs higher accuracy, so new mode - “scanner dose control”
\- estimates energy of next pulse, based on energy of previous pulses
\- generates set point for next pulse
--[p482]-- This width may differ between systems.
(The /500 has a smaller effective slit width than a /400. The /700 slit is again smaller than the /500.) On a /500 the effective slit width is 7.34 mm on wafer level.
The intensity profile is s-dependent. For a smaller s, the profile is more straight. For larger s, the profile is more sloped. The s-variation induces a rotation of the sidewall about its half-height value, so s does not influence the effective slit width.
--[p482]-- For the /500 and up, dose uniformity typically requires a minimum of 30 laser pulses on each part of the die to get a sufficient dose uniformity.
 Pulse-to-Pulse variation (20%) Dose Variation:
 Pulse Energy needs Control
 a. Laser Dose Control: average pulse energy varies too much
 b. Scanner Dose Control: MA reduced ca. factor 10
 Scanner measures pulse energy (in AERIAL illuminator)
 Scanner calculates Moving Average of N pulses in slit

===== [p370-370] An Introduction to the PAS 5500 Software FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Test Manager (3块) =====
--[p370]-- The tests are arranged by subsystem.
2 - Modify Test Queue: It is possible to set up a “Test Queue” to handle a series of tests. Automated sequences can be modified here. More than one test queue can be defined
3 - Run Test Queue: This feature is used to start any of the defined test queues.
4 - View Test Reports: The software generates a report when tests are carried out. They can be viewed form this location.
--[p370]-- They can be viewed form this location.
5 - Test Queue Utilities: Typical file utilities such as copying, renaming, deleting can be carried out with test queues from this location.
--[p370]-- The above slid shows the Test Manager main menu. A large number of tests are defined in the PAS software. This feature will be used extensively during the practical part of the course. Thus the menu items are described below.
0 - Exit: As usual this button returns the user to the previous menu.
1 - Run Test: This brings the user to a screen where the specific tests to be run can be chosen. The tests are arranged by subsystem.

===== [p341-344] Introduction to semiconductor industry FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Resist tone: (15块) =====
--[p344]-- The quartz is covered on one side with a chrome layer, which blocks 99% of the exposure light.
Where there’s no chrome, the light is transmitted. The patterns on the reticle are written using E-beam (electron beam) writers. (This is a lithographic process much like the wafer lithographic process: reticle is covered with resist, E-beam writes in resist, resist is developed, chrome is etched, resist is removed.)
To keep dust away from the reticle, both sides can be covered with a pellicle, which is a thin frame around the image area, on top of which a transparent film is stretched.
--[p344]-- If the reticle data file is not available, the default lay-out of the reticle can be used.
Note that in the latter case, the Ebeam positioning errors will not be taken into account.
Reticles are stored in a SMIF pod (one reticle) or SMIF box (six reticles, picture on slide). These boxes are a semiconductor standard. SMIF stands for Standard Mechanical InterFace.
--[p342]-- ASML’s PAS 5500 /900 is designed to print features of 0.15 micrometer, the AT systems are to eventually carry this specification below 0.1 micrometer.
Wafers are thin slices of silicon, polished flat.
The sizes as mentioned on the slide are the better known standards these days.
The silicon is grown as a single crystal rod. The rod is slowly rotating in a bath of silicon, while it’s being pulled out. The wafers are cut from the silicon rod and polished flat.
--[p342]-- The picture is a side view of the exposure of the photo-active coating on a wafer.
Only a small part of the wafer is displayed. Note that the projection lens inverts the reticle image.
‘Scanning’ means that during the illumination of the mask, the mask moves over the lens while the wafer moves underneath. On Step & repeat systems mask and wafer do not move during exposure.
Illuminating the wafer through mask and lens is often referred to as ‘exposing’. During the ‘exposure’, the resist coating on the wafer is exposed to the light that passed through the features on the mask.
--[p342]-- The mask was as large as the wafer and contained several dies.
The entire wafer was exposed in a single scan.
From those, (it is beyond the scope of this text to state precisely why) industry has switched to 5 (5 times image size reduction from mask to wafer) projection printing systems that are called steppers. The mask now contains one or only a few dies. The wafer is divided in an imaginary matrix of square cells, all of which are exposed in sequence. ASML steppers are officially called Step & Repeat systems.
--[p342]-- Step & Scan systems squeeze the maximum possible performance from the projection lens, and will gradually replace existing steppers in the semiconductor industry.
PAS 5500 Step & Scan systems evolved on the old stepper frame, and continue to improve. ASML’s new AT systems are the next generation in projection printing systems, and have the potential of further reducing possible feature size.
--[p344]-- Before exposure, the ID given here is checked with the bar-code read on each reticle.
If the correct reticle is present, it can be selected and used.
Each reticle comes with a data file that contains the precise locations of the structures on the reticle. During tests it is sometimes of critical importance to know these locations. After exposure, during a readout or modelling sequence, the software will ask the user to provide the location information. If the reticle data file is not available, the default lay-out of the reticle can be used.
--[p342]-- The result of a successful exposure is that the mask image is ‘transferred’ into the resist.
Step & Repeat systems v.s. Step & Scan systems:
After proximity printing, 1 (1 reduction means: no image size reduction from mask to wafer.) scanning projection printing systems became popular. These systems used reflective optics and a scanning illumination slit to project the images from a ‘1‘ mask onto the wafer. The mask was as large as the wafer and contained several dies.
--[p341]-- A positive tone resist is a photo-resist in which the exposed part is washed away (developed) by a specific solvent, while the unexposed part stays to form a barrier for any process step that follows. With negative tone resists, the exposed part stays on the wafer to form a barrier, while the unexposed part is washed away.
It is common terminology to speak of a (wafer) stepper or (wafer) scanner.
The picture is a side view of the exposure of the photo-active coating on a wafer.
--[p342]-- ASML steppers are officially called Step & Repeat systems.
ASML’s last stepper, the PAS 5500 /300, can reliably print features of 0.25 micrometer.
To be able to further decrease the feature size, scanning systems were designed. Basically these are steppers with ‘scanning masks and wafers’. ASML ‘scanners’ are officially called Step & Scan systems and should not be confused with the older 1 scanning systems.
--[p343]-- The wafers are cut from the silicon rod and polished flat.
To mark the crystalline axis, a notch or flat edge is created, which can henceforth be used to (re)produce the correct angular position of the wafer.
To cycle wafers through the machine, ASML Step & Scan systems are equipped with a wafer handling system. It accepts wafer cassettes, which may contain up to 25 wafers. The wafer handling system can also be connected directly to a wafer track (coater/developer machine), in which case the cassettes are obsolete.
--[p344]-- This is the positioning of the wafer with respect to the reticle.
The bar-code, 12NC code and the name of the reticle are for identification purposes. The first is for identification by the step & scan system (the bar-code is always read when a reticle is removed from a library), the latter two for visual identification by the user. When preparing an exposure sequence, the software requires the user to provide the ID of the reticle that is to be used. Before exposure, the ID given here is checked with the bar-code read on each reticle.
--[p344]-- Reticles are the tooling by which circuit design is transferred to the wafer. For ASML equipment, the features on a reticle are either 4 times or 5 times the size on wafer level. A reticle is a square quartz plate. Most used today is the 6 inches square reticle. The thickness may vary per reticle and is of the order of several mm. The next standard for reticles will be 9 inches square.
The quartz is covered on one side with a chrome layer, which blocks 99% of the exposure light.
--[p341]-- The photo-active coating is commonly called ‘photo-resist’ or just ‘resist’. Resists are typically wavelength selective, as we do not want stray light to expose or change it. The selectivity can be distributed over a wide range of wavelengths, however. Useful resists will be insensitive to any wavelength (except the exposure light’s wavelength) that occurs in the area where wafers with undeveloped resist on them are being used.
--[p344]-- The purpose of the pellicle is to make sure that dust particles are not projected in focus, and will thus not directly have a killing effect on yield (the amount of good chips per wafer).
ASML has requirement for special reticle marks for positioning. The pre-alignment marks are for coarse positioning. They are used for placing the reticle on the reticle stage. The alignment marks are for fine positioning. This is the positioning of the wafer with respect to the reticle.

===== [p141-141] Stage Movement Design > Cogging (1块) =====
--[p141]--  Long stroke force varies with position due to:
\- magnetization of the metal near the coils,
\- highly localized magnetic fields.
Electromagnets (coils)
Permanent magnets
Solution (reduce effect): Delocalize magnetic fields by placing the permanent magnets at an angle.

===== [p166-171] LEVEL SENSOR (LS) FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Level Sensor (LS) (17块) =====
--[p166]-- The level sensor consists of 2 systems: the red and the white system. Based on the measurements with the red and white level sensor, the three level actuators (cam-disks) underneath the mirror block are moved to correct the wafer height and tilt.
The slide displays the layout of the red system. The red system uses an infrared laser (820 nm) and has a large capture range ( 300 µm). The spot size of the red laser spot is about 1 x 1 mm on wafer level (see next slide).
--[p168]-- This spot is always projected on the center of an exposure field or the center of the lens. Due to the reflective wafer or lens surface, the spot is reflected and projected onto the detection aperture. The detection aperture blocks part of the light. This results in two spots at different heights, one for each section of the bi-cell. The bicell detects light intensity on two segments. From these two segments, the difference signal and sum signal are measured.
--[p167]-- Both beams are reflected by the wafer/lens and hit a bi-cell.
Three level actuators raise or lower the wafer to get it at nominal height If the wafer is at the correct height, the difference signal from the bi-cell is zero.
The red beam is projected through a projection aperture. This aperture defines a lightspot on wafer level of 1 mm by 1 mm (where the projection aperture is 1mm by 0.1mm). This enlargement of the spot is caused by the small angle between the level sensor and the wafer/lens.
--[p169]-- So we correct for this by changing the beam position on the bicell, making the difference signal zero. This is done by the reference plane plate. If we wouldn’t correct for the measurement beam as well, the wafer would now be exposed out of focus. Therefore we have a similar reference plane plate in the measurement beam. This plane plate is moving in exactly the same way is the other one (they are both attached to the same metal frame which is moved).
--[p170]-- With these 4 spots it is possible to measure height and tilt in the slit.
The detectors for the white system are two quadcells. A quadcell is a light sensitive device, with 4 separate segments. One quadcell detects both P reference beams and both P measurement beams. The other quadcell is doing the same for the Q beams. For every beam, best height is detected when maximum intensity is measured by each segment of the quadcell. This is explained in the following slides.
--[p170]-- The leveling system works equally well on all process layers.
The white system has a small capture range of about  23 µm. The white level sensor is the system that is used for the actual height and tilt measurements. The white system also has measurement and reference beams. The white system has 2 branches (P and Q) which each create 2 spots, so in total there are 4 spots (P1, P2, Q1 and Q2). These 4 spots overlap partially creating a measured area of about 26 x 8 mm on wafer level (see picture).
--[p170]-- The maximum reflectivity is at the top surface, independent of process layers.
The white system uses a halogen lamp as light source. This light is filtered so that effectively the red part of the spectrum is used (725 - 950 nm). The reason for having a broad band light source is that the measurement becomes process insensitive. The thin film interference effects are averaged over a large wavelength range. The leveling system works equally well on all process layers.
--[p168]-- From these two segments, the difference signal and sum signal are measured.
The difference signal on the bi-cell represents the offset from the center of the bi-cell. Best height is measured when the difference signal is zero. The sum signal represents the light intensity (used to compensate for fluctuations in the laser or reflectivity of the wafer). If the difference signal is not zero, the wafer height is changed by moving the three level actuators underneath the mirror block.
This slide shows more detail about the red level sensor.
--[p171]-- The average height measured on the 4 spots is the height of the wafer surface.
The difference between the four spots is used to determine the tilt of the wafer surface in X and Y direction.
In the white system, the same reference and offset plane plates are active as in the red system.
Note: The explanation on this slide is an simplification of the functioning of the white level sensor. The slide explains enough to understand the basic principle and the exact details are out of the scope of this presentation.
--[p170]-- In this picture you can see that the red and white system are integrated.
The level sensor is divided over two branches: P and Q. The P branch contains both the red and white system, the Q branch only has the white system. The two branches are oriented in a 45° orientation to the X- and Y-axes. This reduces the dependency on die structure orientation, because the structure layout is mostly oriented along the X- and Y-axes. For both red and white systems only the surface is measured because of the very shallow angle of incidence.
--[p169]-- The reference plane plates are mounted in the detection side of the laser beams.
In the projection side of the measurement beam, there is another plane plate. This is the offset plane plate. By using the offset plane plate the operator can give a pre-defined focus-offset to deliberately change the wafer height. This can for instance be done in job definition or batch control. The offset plane plate is also used to calibrate the level sensor.
--[p169]-- The offset plane plate is also used to calibrate the level sensor.
If a wafer is exposed and it appears to be exposed out of focus, then the level sensor was not properly calibrated (the offset plane plate tilt was incorrect). We should perform a test to calibrate focus in this case. Normally focus is calibrated at least once per day. The tilt of the offset plane plate is better known as the machine constant: level sensor height.
This is an overview of the whole level sensor system. In this picture you can see that the red and white system are integrated.
--[p166]-- Level sensor 48142 v00, 25/04/00 3
The level sensor is a measurement unit, mounted in the sensor plate and has to measure the distance and tilt between the bottom of the lens and the top of the wafer. Based on these measurements, the field on the wafer that has to be exposed will be brought in the focal plane of the lens. The level sensor uses a light source, which is projected on the wafer and lens, and detectors which are used to measure the reflected signals.
--[p171]-- This is explained in the following slides.
A 100 µm-pitch grating is projected under a small angle (with respect to the wafer) for each of the four spots (P1, P2, Q1 and Q2). The reflected signal is projected on the detection side where it passes through a reference 100 µm grating. The quadcell measures the transmitted light. Best height is detected when the measured intensity is maximal. The average height measured on the 4 spots is the height of the wafer surface.
--[p169]-- This slide shows more detail about the red level sensor.
Besides the lasers, reference and measurement beams and the bi-cells, you can also see some plane plates. A plane plate is a piece of optics that, depending on the incoming light beam angle, will displace the outgoing beam.
The reference plane plates are used to correct for drift in the reference beam. If the bi-cell in the reference beam, detects a difference signal that is not equal to zero, this cannot be corrected for by moving the lens up or down.
--[p166]-- The purpose of the level sensor system is to accurately determine the distance and tilt between the wafer and the projection lens.
 The main components of the LS system are:
\- P and Q branch
\- red system
\- white system
The main actions of the LS system are: - evaluate the red and white beams on the detector cells - activate the three level actuator motors of the wafer stage

ASML CONFIDENTIAL
--[p167]-- The spot size of the red laser spot is about 1 x 1 mm on wafer level (see next slide).
The only purpose of the red system is to bring the wafer in the capture range of the white system.
For measuring the distance between the lens and the wafer, two beams are needed. The reference beam is projected on the bottom of the projection lens. The measurement beam is projected on the surface of the wafer. Both beams are reflected by the wafer/lens and hit a bi-cell.

===== [p172-180] LEVEL SENSOR (LS) FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Level Sensor System Layout (18块) =====
--[p180]-- So when a part of an edge field lies beyond the wafer edge, the level control software automatically switches from All\_By\_LS, where both height and tilt are under level sensor control; i.e. taking into account signals from all four spots, updating tilt as well as height information, to Z\_By\_LS, where only the height is under level sensor control; NOT taking into account (corrupted) signals from one or more spots, which are not measuring on the wafer any more.
--[p173]-- This correction is the stone to lens tilt (see next page).
The stone to lens tilt is a machine constant that has to be calibrated with a special test.
With field-by-field leveling the height and tilt for every field that is to be exposed, is measured. To do this, only the white system is used since the wafer was already in the capture range of white after global leveling. During this leveling we measure the height and tilt of the field with respect to the level sensor.
--[p177]-- This is called on the fly leveling.
The measurements are taken dynamically at a sample rate which has been increased by a factor of 10 compared to the stepper. This provides enough band width for the focus and leveling servo control system to make continuous corrections for wafer height and tilt variations, at the maximum wafer scan speed of 250mm/sec.
A scan needed to expose a field consists of several parts.
•The exposure scan length is determined by the field that is to be exposed. So this length can vary between 1 and 33 mm.
--[p180]-- Then only height (Z-) information is updated from the remaining spots, and the tilt is extrapolated from previous measurements.
--[p173]-- During global leveling the height of the wafer is measured on three points.
The X, Y position of these three points is defined in the machine constants. The red system is used to bring the wafer surface in the capture range of the white system. Then the average height measured by the four white spots is stored for each of the three points. The average of these three points is the average wafer height. The difference between these three is the tilt in X and tilt in Y. To be able to measure these three points the wafer stage has to move in X and Y.
--[p173]-- To be able to measure these three points the wafer stage has to move in X and Y.
During this movement the wafer stage follows the stone surface. If there is a tilt difference between the stone and the focal plane, this can not be measured during global leveling. If you wouldn’t correct for this tilt difference, the wafer would not be in the focal plane after global leveling. Therefore we have to apply a correction during global leveling to make sure that the wafer surface is in the focal plane. This correction is the stone to lens tilt (see next page).
--[p178]-- So this length can vary between 1 and 33 mm.
•The slit scan is needed to guarantee a proper dose over the whole exposed field. There are 2 slit scans, 1 before and 1 after the exposure scan (for dose control reasons). The slit scan length is determined by the effective slit width of the system. The slit scan length is half the slit width. The effective slit width for the current systems are:
• /400 13 mm /900 9.6 mm /5x0 7.3 mm /700 5.3 mm
•The prescan is determined by the time needed to synchronize the stages.
--[p179]-- So to be able to expose a Z\_By\_LS or None\_By\_LS part, we need reference information from another part of the wafer that was exposed in All\_By\_LS mode.
Note that this level sensor functionality has to be known for the exposure scan, the slit scan and also the pre-scan. That means that compared to a stepper the area that is to be measured by the level sensor is much larger.
On a scanner the level sensor functionality can change within an exposure field.
--[p175]-- In the top picture the level sensor tilt machine constant is zero.
This means that after field-by-field leveling, the wafer is parallel to the level sensor plane. We want the wafer to be in the focal plane after field-by-field leveling. So we have to correct for the sensor plate position by using the level sensor tilt machine constant as can be seen in the bottom picture.
Unlike with a stepper the field-by-field leveling measurement can not be static. Since the field is dynamically exposed, the leveling measurement also has to be dynamic.
--[p172]-- This is a front view of the sensor plate. The orientation of P (right back to left front) and Q (left back to right front) branch can be seen with respect to the coordinate system. Also the shallow angle of the level sensor branches with respect to the wafer and lens surface is visible.
Wafer leveling is done in two steps: global and field-by-field leveling.
During global leveling the height of the wafer is measured on three points.
--[p173]-- During this leveling we measure the height and tilt of the field with respect to the level sensor.
It is very likely that the level sensor is not parallel to the focal plane, so we would make an error. Therefore we have to apply a correction during field-by-field leveling to make sure that the wafer surface is in the focal plane. This correction is the level sensor tilt. The level sensor tilt is a machine constant that has to be calibrated with a special test.

--[p179]-- That means that these spots are invalid and will not contribute to the height and tilt measurement.
If all 4 white spots are valid, we can determine height and tilt and call this All\_By\_LS. If only 1,2 or 3 spots are valid we can not determine the tilt, but only the height. We call this Z\_By\_LS. If no spots are valid we can neither determine height nor tilt and we call this None\_By\_LS. Although we can not measure the Z\_By\_LS and None\_By\_LS parts, we still would like to expose these parts of the wafer in focus and with the correct tilt.
--[p174]-- On the slide the sequence of global leveling is shown.
In the first 3 pictures the stone to lens tilt machine constant is zero. This means that after global leveling, the mirror block is parallel to the stone. We want the mirror block to be in the focal plane after global leveling. So we have to correct for the mirror block position by using the stone to lens tilt machine constant as can be seen in the bottom 3 pictures.
On this slide the result of field-by-field leveling is shown. In the top picture the level sensor tilt machine constant is zero.
--[p177]-- Since the field is dynamically exposed, the leveling measurement also has to be dynamic.
There are basically two ways to do this. You can measure the height and tilt first during a measurement scan and after that expose the field during an exposure scan. This will have a big negative effect on throughput (every field has to be scanned twice). Instead we measure the height and tilt at the same time as we expose the field. That means that the height and tilt in the field are measured and corrected continuously during the scan. This is called on the fly leveling.
--[p180]-- On a scanner the level sensor functionality can change within an exposure field.
In the beginning of the scan a field can be All\_By\_LS and during the scan it can change to Z\_By\_LS. For the All\_By\_LS part the level sensor is in control of the level actuators to correct local wafer height and tilt. For the Z\_By\_LS part the tilt can not be measured. But still we want to expose the remaining part of the field with the correct tilt. That means we need reference information to feed-forward the tilt in the Z\_By\_LS part.
--[p178]-- •The prescan is determined by the time needed to synchronize the stages.
The prescan length = (WS\_switch\_settling\_time + AGC\_settling\_time) \* (maximum\_scan\_speed) In practice:
Wafer stage switch settling time: 50ms AGC settling time: 5 ms
Maximum scan speed: 250mm/sec.
So the prescan length is 13.75 mm. At the moment this value is used for all systems.
The total scan length needed for exposing a 33 mm field therefore can be 33 + 13 + 14 = 60 mm.
Then there is also a certain length needed to slow down the stages.
--[p178]-- Then there is also a certain length needed to slow down the stages.
This is normally not taken into account because exposure dose and leveling in that area are not relevant.
We have to make a distinction between different level sensor functionality's. This functionality is depending on how many of the 4 white spots are valid. It is possible that on the edge of the wafer 1 or more white spots fall off the valid wafer surface. That means that these spots are invalid and will not contribute to the height and tilt measurement.
--[p180]-- That means we need reference information to feed-forward the tilt in the Z\_By\_LS part.
The feedforward of the tilt is then monitored by using the LVDTs underneath the mirror block of the wafer stage. In this case the feed-forward information is taken from within the field, because in the All\_By\_LS part the tilt was already measured. That means that for this field, the scan direction is fixed. We can only scan up, since if we would scan down, the reference information that is needed to expose the first Z\_By\_LS part is not available.

The normal pre-alignment sequence is as follows: – one 360º scan ----> checking ----> centering – another 360º scan ----> checking ----> centering (unless first scan is already good enough) – finally a last fine scan (a few -) to determine the position of the notch accurately
The first two steps are repeated until the measured error is within a certain window. This re-centering window can be set in MC’s between 5-200 µm.
--[p111]-- During this action the expected mark position and the actual measured mark position are compared.
The offset between these two is used during global alignment to find the marks at their actual position. The video output of the mark sensor camera is visible on the operator control.
When the wafer is cycled through the machine, it is clamped by vacuum on the following components: Pedestal (if track is used)
--[p111]-- The mark sensor consists of a halogen light source illuminating the wafer surface and a CCD camera detecting the reflected signal. To prevent exposure of the photo-resist, the light is filtered through a red filter (> 600 nm). The camera will optical recognize the alignment marks. For this optical pre-alignment we need 2 alignment marks with large edge clearance in opposite regions. During this action the expected mark position and the actual measured mark position are compared.
--[p111]-- This re-centering window can be set in MC’s between 5-200 µm.
The default value is 100 µm. More detail about this in the coordinate systems presentation. The actual number of scans is dependent on the initial error and the required accuracy.
The mark sensor can be used if the wafer that is to be loaded contains alignment marks (usage is optional). In this case, optical pre-alignment with the mark sensor will be performed after mechanical pre-alignment.

===== [p279-288] REFLECTION IMAGE SENSOR (RIS) > Image Sensor Working Principle (10块) =====
--[p280]-- The intensity of the reflected light depends on how well the projected images match the marks on the wafer stage RIS plate
By comparing the amount of light reflected from the 3 RIS marks simultaneously, the system can calculate the relative position, rotation, magnification, height and tilt of the aerial image relative to the wafer stage.
This slide shows the layout of the Standard Reticle.
--[p279]--  Image sensor scan:
– Image special marks, measure aerial image position.
– Three positions define image plane.
 Usage:
– Determine best focal plane
– Set Image Sensor reference
– Keep track of drift
When a machine is tuned to its optimum, this state is stored in the machine constants: the reference state of the machine.
Then an Image sensor scan is performed to define the reference state for the Image Sensor.
--[p279]-- Then an Image sensor scan is performed to define the reference state for the Image Sensor.
During such a scan the stage makes a Z-scan and the Image Sensor stores the ideal aerial image. After that you can do Image Sensor scans to maintain this aerial image. The machine will keep track of the focal plane drift.
The RIS uses the (Deep) Ultra Violet light of the illuminator to project 3 special RIS marks from the standard reticle (also known as reticle stage RIS plate) through the projection lens onto the RIS marks on the Wafer Stage RIS plate.
--[p288]-- In order to control the quality of the projected image with an IS, a set of Image Quality (IQ) machine constants has been created.
Reference data. These are defined during system setup and calibration tests. The reference machine constants will not be changed by an image sensor scan. The image sensor can modify the drift machine constants only to maintain the optimal imaging performance of the PAS 5500.
Image sensor reference data.

===== [p82-84] FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Position Control (7块) =====
--[p82]-- During set up of the scanner, the airmounts sub-system is calibrated.
A look up table is created detailing the forces acting on each airmount for every stage position. By reference to this calibration data the system is able to apply the correct forces, via the airmount motors, to counter the effect of stage movement. This is known as: “feed forward correction”.
The mainplate must be kept within a vertical position range.
--[p84]-- These shortcuts will typically ruin the dynamic performance of the system and introduce MA and MSD problems (discussed in dynamic image quality).
The airmounts them selves can be used to diagnose problems with dynamic shortcuts as part of complex service procedures.
--[p84]-- Defined Shortcut - necessary connections to supply facilities from external world to silent world
Dynamic Shortcut - unwanted and undefined connections
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
Ideally the silent world would be totally isolated from the external world. However, components in the silent world require facilities like water, vacuum and power. The connections supplying facilities introduce defined shortcuts.
--[p83]-- The temperature of each motor is monitored.
If it exceeds 65 degrees Celsius, the system will generate a warning message:
sanity check error: Motor XX NOT OK where XX is the motor number (0 to 5)
The airmounts have a mechanical end stop to limit the range the piston plate makes, both horizontal and vertical. Airmounts are very powerful, and can easily lift a weight of 15000 N each!
A shortcut/crossover is a connection between the silent world and the external world.
Two Types:
--[p82]-- Feed forward correction by airmounts to maintain equilibrium during scans.
 External vibrations are isolated.
The stages have significant masses. As they scan, the forces acting on the airmounts are continually changing (e.g. moving centre of gravity). Each airmount is equipped with a horizontal and a vertical motor. The motors work together to prevent the mainplate moving. During set up of the scanner, the airmounts sub-system is calibrated.
--[p83]-- The mainplate must be kept within a vertical position range.
If the mainplate vertical position is out of this 65 µm window (error window vertical), a warning message will be displayed in the error logging window:
sanity check error: VERTICAL NOT OK The vertical range of the airmounts is set to 0 ±200 µm by the mechanical hard stop mentioned earlier.
If the Lorentz motors are defective, or if there is a problem with the sensors, the motors can oscillate. This will cause them to overheat. The temperature of each motor is monitored.
--[p84]-- The connections supplying facilities introduce defined shortcuts.
Dynamic shortcuts can occur for a variety of reasons (e.g. a cable trapped between the wafer stage stone and the mainframe). Such shortcuts degrade system performance in a number of ways.
The impact of dynamic shortcuts between the moving parts of the silent world (scanning reticle stage and scanning wafer stage) and any other part of the system will be the largest.

===== [p48-49] FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > ATHENA: (2块) =====
--[p49]-- AERIAL stands for Automatic Enhancement Resolution Imaging for Advanced Lithography.
The purpose of the illuminator is to create a spot of light at reticle level that is highly uniform and has coherence properties as created/modified in the pupil shaping optics.
The slide displays an impression of the AERIAL illuminator and the projection lens. In the picture, the basic parts of the illumination section are indicated. Note that for a /400 the light source is a lamp, approximately located in the space of the beam steering unit.
--[p48]-- ATHENA stands for: Advanced Technology using High order ENhanced Alignment.
The PAS 5500 version of ATHENA combines TTL and OA alignment. The TTL system is used to align wafer stage to reticle stage and wafer stage to reticle. OA can then be used (instead of TTL) to align wafer to wafer stage. Thus wafer, reticle and both scan axes are properly aligned.
AERIAL stands for Automatic Enhancement Resolution Imaging for Advanced Lithography.

===== [p13-17] Wavelength vs. Depth of Focus (2) > ASML CONFIDENTIAL (9块) =====
--[p17]-- As the NA is reduced, more and more of the first order information is masked.
As a result, it becomes more and more difficult for the process to resolve an image. There is, however, an advantage to reducing NA under some circumstances, and that point will be discussed in detail later in this presentation.
--[p13]-- At the focal plane, all orders captured by the lens are recombined to form a replica of the reticle image at the magnification of the lens. Notice that for the zero order, the size of the lens is not an issue. This part of the light information passes through the lens essentially parallel to the optical axis. But the zeroth order information only defines the body of the image on the reticle.
--[p16]-- It is a misconception, for example, to visualize illumination of the reticle as coming from only one spot, or from just two directions, etc. As a general statement, most of the graphics in this presentation are to convey what is occurring in terms of single rays of illumination and how those rays are oriented either with each other, with respect to the illuminator, or how they have been affected by passing through the reticle at a single point.
--[p16]-- Therefore the reticle feature is the smallest feature which can be fully resolved by this lens, using conventional illumination. Notice that the order information occupies a relatively large area in each case. This is directly related to the high sigma illumination; that is, a (comparatively) large range of (directional) collimation angle illumination is present.
Here we see an illustration of the extreme case discussed in the previous slide.
--[p16]-- ASML
This slide is of great importance as issues of illumination and lens properties are investigated. “Focus planes that are conjugate with components of the illumination system are out of focus at the reticle and wafer.”
In order to rationalize separated orders at the lens entrance pupil (as in the example drawing), and to help visualize what is happening in the illumination optics and in the projection lens, accepting this statement is very important.
--[p17]-- Here we see an illustration of the extreme case discussed in the previous slide.
The bottom lens is not large enough, meaning high enough numerical aperture, to capture any of the first order information from the reticle feature. As a result, there will only be a DC illumination level at the wafer and no image will be resolved (as if the wafer was well out of focus).
Even if the top lens were used, it is important to consider what will happen if the numerical aperture is set to a lower value. As the NA is reduced, more and more of the first order information is masked.
--[p13]-- But the zeroth order information only defines the body of the image on the reticle.
The first order (and higher) defines the edge position of the image on the reticle, and is therefore critical to the ability to define features with very well controlled dimensions. Large reticle features will resolve more easily and with better control than smaller features, because it is possible for a lens to capture and focus higher order information from those images. Key to the performance of our equipment, however, is in the ability to capture and focus the smallest features.
--[p16]-- The illustration shows how the -1, 0 & +1 orders propagate through a simple lens. The entrance pupil is assumed to be at the aperture plane. Here we see partially collimated illumination, represented by the illumination arriving at the reticle image over some range of angles. At the lens aperture, the zeroth and first order information from the reticle image can be seen to fill the aperture.
--[p13]-- To illustrate how the diffraction angle of first order information is redirected by a simple lens, this example shows the paths taken through the lens (in two dimensions). The (imaginary) lens used for this example is large enough (high enough Numerical Aperture) to capture all of the first order diffracted information from the reticle image and redirect it to converge at the focal plane.

===== [p143-145] Stage Movement Design > The HP Interferometer System (2/2) (6块) =====
--[p145]-- The plates and the air in between (which serves as a dielectric) form a plate capacitor.
The electronics attached to the plates measure the capacitance.
Changing the distance between the plates changes the capacitance. Once the capacitive sensor has been calibrated, the measured capacitance is a measure of the distance d.
Note that the dielectric properties of the air between the plates needs to be well known and constant. A change in air humidity, for instance, will change the dielectric properties of the air, causing a measured height offset that is not physically present.
Since the C&T system controls humidity, this is a hypothetical point.
--[p144]-- (In this case horizontal.
To pinpoint a position in an xy plane, two sensors are necessary, one arranged in the x direction, and one in the y direction.
Measure capacitance of plate capacitor as a function of the distance d.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
The capacitive sensor measures the distance between two parallel plates. The measurement precision is in the order of (a few) microns.
The plates and the air in between (which serves as a dielectric) form a plate capacitor.
--[p143]-- Reflections from a rotated stage:
Reflection from mirror.
Reflection from corner cube.
Double reflection from mirror via corner cube (restores beam parallelism).
Corner cube: (3D illustration)
Scanning Stages 48142 v00, 24/08/00 32
For the interferometer to function, we need to get the measurement beam to interfere with the reference beam at the detector. To get this interference, we need to control the direction of the beams.
--[p144]-- The sensor will output a signal proportional to the difference between these two field strengths. When the Hall sensor output is zero, it will be centered underneath the magnet: that is where both halves will detect the same magnetic field strength.
The Hall sensors ASML uses have a typical reproducibility (of zero position) of 2 µm .
Note that the position is only determined in one axis. (In this case horizontal.
--[p143]-- Note that in practise the optical component layouts may vary.
They might include other optical components as well, but the final goal will remain as discussed. The minimum size of the components is determined by the laser beam diameter.
The interferometer beam has a Gaussian intensity distribution and 98% of the intensity is within a diameter of 9mm.
The Hall sensor consists of two parts that measure the strength of the magnetic field (I.e. the magnetic field line density).
--[p143]-- To get this interference, we need to control the direction of the beams.
If the stage mirror is not perpendicular to the beam, the beam direction will change (left picture). The measurement beam spot at the detector will start to shift away from the reference beam spot. For larger angles the measurement beam will be less able to interfere with the reference beam.
By making use of corner cube components (middle picture), the problem is solved (right picture).
Note that in practise the optical component layouts may vary.

===== [p140-140] Stage Movement Design > Lorentz Motor (3块) =====
--[p140]--  Working principle:
– Opposite magnetic poles attract,
– Equal magnetic poles repel.
Use permanent magnets and coils to induce magnetic fields and control the current through the coils to control the attracting or repelling force.
The slide indicates how movement is induced by a Lorentz motor.
From the simple principle it becomes evident that the Lorentz motor has a limited range of movement, magnets are moved closer together or further apart.
--[p140]-- The stages must be moveable over ranges of typically several decimeters.
To make this possible a linear motor is used. The principle of the linear motor is explained on the next two slides.
The linear motor is set up in a way that essentially removes the movement range limitation.
--[p140]-- Force is induced as soon as a voltage is applied to the electromagnet. So in a static state, no voltage is applied to the electromagnet.
Since magnetic forces act on a distance, the bodies do not need to touch. This is very good for vibration isolation.
The Lorentz motor can be made very powerful (fast) and very accurate. However, for performing a scan this principle is less useful because of the limited movement range. The stages must be moveable over ranges of typically several decimeters.

===== [p141-141] Stage Movement Design > Definition of cogging: (1块) =====
--[p141]-- The fluctuation of the long stroke force depending on the relative position of both arrays of magnets.
Why cogging is a problem:
Stage position and speed control is difficult because stage acceleration would be non-constant.

===== [p295-303] TRANSMISSION IMAGE SENSOR (TIS) > Hardware Configuration (TIS compared to RIS) (13块) =====
--[p301]-- Because the different step and scan systems (/400 and /500) have different size exposure slits, the standard reticle contains sets of marks at different locations, to ensure that for each system there will always be 5 marks available.
Note: /500, /550, /700, /750, /900, /950 use the same mark locations.
On steppers, five TIS marks have been added to the current COMBI reticle which also contains the RIS marks.
--[p298]-- During this scan the intensity of the aerial image is measured and the results are stored.
If the transmission mark is perfectly overlapping the aerial image, the detector measures maximum intensity.
Therefore the main differences in the operating principles of TIS and RIS are:
– TIS performs a direct intensity measurement on a single aerial image, with the calculated image position having an absolute value. No reflected images are measured with this system.
--[p303]-- The ratio mark (centre segment) is used for normalising the measured TIS signals on each TIS x- or y- segment in order to compensate for fluctuations in the intensity of the illumination source (a form of ‘white balance’ setting).
To calculate the Image Quality parameters (focus, magnification, translation, rotation and image tilt) with TIS, at least five TIS marks projected from the standard reticle must be measured.
--[p303]-- The mark comprises five different sections called segments.
There are two horizontal and two vertical segments, and a centre segment. The horizontal and vertical segments both have one vertical set and one horizontal set of lines. To distinguish between them, we use the abbreviations Txh, Tyh for horizontal, and Txv, Tyv for vertical. The dimensions of the segments were chosen such that the effective open area of each pair of x- and ysegments is the same (e.g. Txh and Txv have the same area).
--[p302]-- The TIS mark positions were chosen such that the reproducibility of the image quality output parameters (focus, tilt, translation, magnification, rotation, field curvature, astigmatism and third order distortion) were optimised. Note that currently on steppers, the last three of this list are not included in the optimisation.
TIS Mark Layout at Wafer Level
This is a detailed view of the TIS mark.
The mark comprises five different sections called segments.
--[p297]-- a previously determined reference state.
The TIS system still projects marks at reticle level through the lens to create an aerial image, but this image is no longer reflected off the wafer stage marks. Instead, a detector mounted underneath the wafer stage marks measures the amount of light transmitted through the wafer stage mark during an image scan.
Hence the different names:
REFLECTION image sensor , and TRANSMISSION Image sensor.
--[p301]-- On step and scan systems, TIS is used in combination with the standard reticle (fixed TIS/RIS plate) mounted in the reticle stage. On this plate RIS, TIS, and alignment marks are present in order to perform image sensor scans.
When performing an IS scan, the reticle stage is static. Therefore only part of the standard reticle is available to the illumination/projection system, because the illumination field is limited by the slit size.
--[p298]-- REFLECTION image sensor , and TRANSMISSION Image sensor.
The wafer stage scans the Image Sensor mark through the aerial image of a TIS mark, projected from the Standard Reticle (TIS/RIS reticle stage plate). The Image Sensor scans the complete aerial image by slicing through one of the five images at several z-levels in order to collect an array of x-y co-ordinates along the the z-axis. The direction of motion per z-level (so: in the x-y plane) is diagonal. During this scan the intensity of the aerial image is measured and the results are stored.
--[p295 [表]]-- RIS | TIS
Principle of operation | Projected IS marks reflectedby IS marks on the wafer stageEach interference patterngenerated is monitored byfour sensorsThus need multiple sensors | Projected IS mark transmittedthrough IS mark on the waferstageTransmitted intensitymeasured by single sensor
Measurement type | Relative | Absolute
Detected Image | Continuous periodic signal | Actual projected image
Sensor location | Beneath sensor plate | Beneath TIS plate, in WS
--[p297]-- However, the way in which the aerial image is measured is very different.
As was explained in the RIS module, the marks from reticle level are projected through the lens, to form an aerial image at wafer level. By scanning an identical set of marks (layout = reticle mark layout, size = reticle mark size times nominal lens magnification) on the wafer stage through the aerial image, and measuring the reflected light, we can determine the relative positions of the aerial image w.r.t. a previously determined reference state.
--[p303]-- As there is only one TIS mark on the TIS sensor module on the wafer stage, the five marks have to be scanned sequentially. Therefore five separate scans must be performed.
Here we see the path of the wafer stage TIS detector through the aerial image during a combined XY and Z scan. The stage scans through the predicted center point of the aerial image at a 45-degree angle, then moves up an incremental distance in Z, then scans back through the predicted center point of the aerial image.
This continues until the system has measured slices through the full Z-scan length defined in the Image Sensor scan setup.
--[p298]-- No reflected images are measured with this system.
– RIS performs an indirect measurement (interference patterns are measured) on multiple aerial images (a continuous periodic signal), with the calculated position having a relative value due to the repeating nature of the periodic wave as the stage is scanned in Z.
The slide shows the location of the Standard Reticle (TIS/RIS plate) on the Reticle Stage. Note that on a current step and scan system, there is no difference in the fiducial plate for RIS or TIS (Both are together on the same plate).
