===== [p346-363] CONTAMINATION & TEMPERATURE CONTROL (C&T) > ASML CONFIDENTIAL (8块) =====
--[p361]-- Helium is a low density gas, so its refractive index changes less for DT or DP, compared to air.
• If the Lens is designed to be air filled, like the DUV lens, synthetic air is used instead of Helium because it has the same refractive index as normal air, and also due to the fact that ozone is created automatically for DUV systems.
• The mixtures are (in volume %):
• Helium mixture (I-line ) : 99.5% He + 0.5% O2
• Synthetic air (DUV) : 80.0% N2 + 20.0% O2
• The Lens Purge air should be ultra clean and very pure. It is supplied in bottles or from the fab supply network.
--[p362]-- It is pressurized using the Clean Air system.
• The purged air is released into the environment (Top module) or removed by the Exhaust Air System (Bottom module).
--[p346]-- In this presentation we will have an overview of the C&T system and discuss all the individual parts of the C&T system.
• Minimize contamination of wafers and reticles in the exposure unit.
• Keep the exposure unit (especially the lens and the wafer and reticle stage compartment) at a stable temperature to achieve sufficient overlay and focus performance.
--[p361]-- • The Lens Purging system provides the lens with a mixture of gasses to protect it from contaminants in the clean room air.
• Traditionally (I-line systems), Helium gas is used. Helium is a low density gas, so its refractive index changes less for DT or DP, compared to air.
--[p347]-- • Very clean and temperature controlled air and temperature controlled water is provided to different components of the exposure unit, also concurrently contaminated or heated air is exhausted from the exposure unit, and nitrogen is supplied to illumination components.
--[p361]-- It is supplied in bottles or from the fab supply network.
• The Gas Control Unit (GCU) controls the gas pressure and flow.
• For i-line systems, the mixture of Gas is led through an ozonizer , where part of O2 becomes ozone (O3).
• Although only a few parts per million of ozone are used, it is a very reactive gas, and is used to create chemical reactions with contaminating particles (eg. Resist particles). The reaction products are removed as a result of the gas flow.
--[p363]-- • Deep UV systems with AERIAL illumination (/300, /500, /700, /900) are purged with Nitrogen.
• The Nitrogen Purge Unit (NPU) supplies Nitrogen to the illumination top and bottom modules, and the beam delivery system.
• In a /900 (193nm), the Nitrogen also avoids a high concentration of ozone in the illumination components. The 193nm light creates ozone from the oxygen that is in the illumination components, causing safety issues and transmission losses.
--[p361]-- The reaction products are removed as a result of the gas flow.
• Due to safety reasons gas exiting the lens is filtered by carbon filter to remove left over ozone before it is released into the environment.
Contamination & Temperature Control 48142 v00, 11/04/00 18
• For i-line systems with AERIAL illumination (/400), air is supplied to the illumination components in the top and bottom module for cooling and particle removal.
• The pressurized air is supplied by the illumination filter box. It is pressurized using the Clean Air system.

===== [p305-307] TRANSMISSION IMAGE SENSOR (TIS) > How Does It Work (11块) =====
--[p307]-- (Note that the RIS mark is also segmented, but the segments are only a small distance apart)
TIS measured data is more accurate because the data points have absolute values, as opposed to relative values as in the case of RIS (i.e. the wafer stage scans the TIS in the x, y, z directions to find the best aligned positions, and there is no cyclic repeat of the same conditions resulting from a grating interference pattern).
--[p305]-- Therefore, we refer to this as an absolute measurement.
RIS performs an indirect measurement (interference patterns are measured) on multiple aerial images (a continuous periodic signal), with the calculated position having a relative value due to the repeating nature of the periodic wave as the stage is scanned in Z (If the drift was very large, we could be one period of). Therefore, we refer to this as a relative measurement.
x, y, z : IS mark position
Zx, Zy : IS mark height
Mag. : Magnification
Rot. : Rotation
ISx, ISy, ISzx, ISzy : IS data
--[p306]-- During the scans, the aerial image profile is measured by means of horizontal wafer stage scans at different z-levels. The highest intensity horizontal position of the single aerial image determines its aligned position in the x- and ydirections. The highest intensity vertical position determines the aligned position of the single aerial image in the z-direction.
--[p307]-- In summary, there are five TIS marks on the Standard Reticle (the RIS system employs three reticle stage RIS marks). Each TIS fiducial mark consists of four segments (Txh, Txv, Tyh and Tyv) and a fifth segment called the ratio mark .
TIS modelling uses more data sets compared with RIS modelling.
Using RIS, x- and y- data is always measured at the same positions, but using TIS, the data can be measured at different locations.
--[p307]-- Image Quality Control software can then process the results obtained from aligning to the aerial image (as opposed to measuring the interference patterns generated by the three IS marks in the RIS system) and compare the measured positions with the initial system values, in order to calculate the system drifts.
Although TIS measures the aerial image in an absolute way, we still need a reference state.
--[p305]-- The above table is an overview of the main differences in the operation of the RIS and TIS systems. The differences will be explained in greater detail later in this presentation. TIS performs a direct intensity measurement on a single aerial image, with the calculated image position having an absolute value. Therefore, we refer to this as an absolute measurement.
--[p305]-- : Rotation
Rx, Ry : Image plane tilt
Tx, Ty : Translation
D3 : Third order distortion
Ast. : Astigmatism
FC : Field curvature
The last three image quality parameters are currently not used in the modelling phase. This will be implemented in future versions of TIS.
Each mark has to be scanned separately to find the best focus position of the single aerial image in the x, y, and z directions.
--[p305 [表]]-- RIS (DUV) | TIS
Which quantities aremeasured | Positions (x, y, Zx, Zy)and intensities (ISx, ISy,ISzx, ISzy) of threeprojected marks. | Positions (x, y, Zx, Zy)and intensities (ISx, ISy,ISzx, ISzy) of fiveprojected marks.
Measurement type | Relative | Absolute
What is modelled | Focus, Mag., Rot., Tx, Ty, Rx, Ry, (D3, Ast., FC).
Capture range (x, y / z) | ± 4μm / ± 8μm | ± 4μm / ± 4μm
IQC scan range(x, y / z) | Single scan:None / ±8μm | Three scan ranges:±0.25μm / ±1μm±0.5μm / ±1.6μm±4μm / ±4μm
--[p307]-- Although TIS measures the aerial image in an absolute way, we still need a reference state.
This because the ‘best focus’ position for the TIS mark will in general differ from the ‘best focus’ position for a resist coated wafer. Thus, we need to know the offset.
--[p306]-- The image sensor measures the intensity of the image (for each segment one detector, so five in total)(maximum intensity occurs when the features of the WS TIS fiducial mark are completely overlapped by the projected image), while the position of the image is determined using the wafer stage position data supplied by the level sensor (and the LVDTs) and the HP interferometers. This results in the absolute position of the aerial image being determined.
--[p306]-- This results in the absolute position of the aerial image being determined.
From these five best aligned x, y, z positions of the aerial images of the five Image Sensor marks, z-related and intra field parameters are calculated by the modelling algorithms. To model the TIS data, two models, an xy-model and a z-model are employed (these models are similar to the RIS models, but field curvature can also be modelled by TIS because of data collected from two additional mark locations).

===== [p90-90] ADVANCED RETICLE MANAGEMENT SYSTEM (ARMS) > ARMS Schematic Overview (5块) =====
--[p90]-- The sequence of events when a reticle is loaded:
•SMIF box is locked to the ARMS frame
•Elevator lowers (with the carrier with reticles) and every slot is scanned for presence of a reticle
•Elevator moves such that the bottom reticle is at the correct height for gripper access
•Gripper moves to the correct library (with the locked SMIF box)
•Gripper moves in the library, underneath the reticle and lifts the reticle
•Plunger is closed
--[p90]-- This will reduce the amount of mechanical stress in the gripper. This because coarse pre-alignment is done with the plunger closed and fine pre-alignment with the plunger open. If the reticle is not seated against all reference points in the gripper, the reticle can slip as the gripper moves from coarse to fine pre-alignment, ruining the coarse pre-alignment performance.
--[p90]-- Gripper puts the reticle back in the carrier, plunger is opened and gripper lifts the reticle again, plunger is closed\*\*
•Gripper retracts from the library and brings reticle to the coarse pre-alignment unit (plunger is closed)
•Reticle is coarse pre-aligned (plunger closed)
•Gripper moves the reticle above the scanning reticle stage load position (fine pre-alignment position)
•Reticle is fine pre-aligned (plunger open)
--[p90]-- •R-pins move up, taking the reticle from the gripper (at this moment the reticle is transferred from the external world to the silent world)
•G-pins retract and the gripper moves slightly to the front
•R-pins move down, loading the reticle on the scanning reticle stage
•Scanning reticle stage moves to exposure position
•Unloading is the opposite sequence
\*\* This “taking the reticle twice” is needed to guarantee the reticle is properly seated against all reference points in the gripper.
--[p90]-- •Gripper moves in the library, underneath the reticle and lifts the reticle
•While the gripper takes the reticle out of the carrier, the barcode is read (plunger is closed)
•Gripper puts the reticle back in the carrier, while the plunger opens
•This is repeated for all reticles (now all reticle ID’s are known)
•Elevator lowers or raises such that the required reticle is at the gripper height •Gripper moves in the library, underneath the reticle, lifts the reticle, plunger is closed.

--[p51]-- An airmount serves as a vibration stop.
It combines an air piston and motion damping apparatus, tuned in such a way that higher frequency oscillations are not fed through the connection.
Motors are present in each airmount to apply force in any direction as is needed to keep the main plate motionless.

--[p155]-- 1) The pre-alignment of the wafer is done at the pre-alignment unit.

2) Even if pre-alignment puts the wafer in the perfect XY position, the wafer still has to be loaded onto the E-chuck. That means that the wafer will be moved after pre-alignment.

--[p155]-- That means that the wafer will be moved after pre-alignment.
This is done by the rotation unit, the dipod and the E-pins. They will introduce an error on top of the pre-alignment result.
So we have to correct for these errors. We can do that by applying static wafer offsets (SWO). These are machine constants that describe the difference between the actual wafer load position and the best wafer load position (according to the Wafer Stage Coordinate System). The static wafer offsets consist of a translation in X and a translation in Y.
--[p154]-- For exposing a zero-layer this is the only alignment possible.
The Wafer Coordinate System (WCS) is the coordinate system as determined by the position of the alignment marks on the wafer. This is measured by the mark sensor during optical pre-alignment and/or by the alignment system during global alignment. For any non-zero layer this is the alignment scenario.
The offset of the GWCS from the WCS is known as the GWCS to WCS offset and consists of translation X, translation Y and rotation Rz.
--[p154]-- For the wafer we specify two coordinate systems instead of just one.
The reason is that we use two different measuring systems to determine the coordinate system on the wafer.
The Geometrical Wafer Coordinate System (GWCS) is the coordinate system as determined by the center and the notch (or flat edge) of the wafer. This is measured by the edge sensor during mechanical pre-alignment. For exposing a zero-layer this is the only alignment possible.

For instance if a customer is using another vendor’s machine which might be the mother machine, the GWCS to WCS offsets maybe used to correct for errors.

===== [p157-159] Stage Movement Design > Dynamic Wafer Offsets (8块) =====
--[p158]-- Wafer is put on E-chuck using SWO, DWO and GWCS-to-WCS-offsets
SWO are the same machine constants. DWO are now measured by the edge sensor. GWCS-to-WCS-offsets are machine constants but should only be used during calibration of the SWO (see page 9).
Besides coordinate systems for wafer and reticle, we also specify coordinate systems for the wafer and reticle stage.
The Reticle stage Zero Coordinate System (RZCS):
--[p157]-- The DWO consists of a translation in X and a translation in Y.
The DWO is added to the scanning wafer stage coordinate system, so this coordinate system is changed. The result is that all wafers will be exposed in the correct position and that alignment marks are put in the center of the capture range of the alignment system. So this is always a software correction.
--[p159]-- This is the coordinate system in which the scanning reticle stage moves after corrections to the RZCS have been applied, for instance after alignment.
Wafer stage Zero Coordinate System (WZCS):
This is the coordinate system in which the wafer stage moves after initialization and therefore only based on mechanical references (zero sensors). This coordinate system is fixed with respect to the lens. When the wafer stage is at its zero position with zero rotation, the WZCS equals the WSCS.
--[p157]-- So this is always a software correction.
In fact, when a wafer is loaded onto the E-chuck, the SWO and DWO are algebraically added together to adjust the scanning wafer stage coordinate system in such a way that all wafers are exposed in the correct position.
Each time a wafer is placed on the E-chuck there are two possibilities:
Mark sensor is used:
Wafer is put on the E-chuck using SWO and DWO
SWO are machine constants so the same for every wafer. DWO are measured by the mark sensor.
Mark sensor is not used:
--[p159]-- When the wafer stage is at its zero position with zero rotation, the WZCS equals the WSCS.
The WZCS origin is always near the lens center.
Wafer Stage Coordinate System (WSCS):
This is the coordinate system in which the wafers stage moves after corrections to the WZCS have been made, for instance after alignment.
The slide displays a graphical representation of the x and y vectors in the Wafer stage coordinate system. For simplicity, we will assume that both reticle stage and wafer stage, have the same z-axis.
From left to right:
--[p157]-- The movement accuracy of the centering unit is limited.
Therefore a window is set in the machine constants (pre-alignment recentering window) that specifies to which accuracy the centering unit has to correct (see also wafer handling presentation). The remaining error as measured by the edge sensor will not be corrected for with the centering unit, but will be stored in the dynamic wafer offsets (DWO). The DWO consists of a translation in X and a translation in Y.
--[p159]-- This is the coordinate system in which the reticle stage moves after initialization and therefore only based on mechanical references (zero sensors).
This coordinate system is fixed with respect to the lens. When the reticle stage is at its zero position with zero rotation, the RZCS equals the RSCS. The RZCS origin is always near the lens center.
Reticle Stage Coordinate System (RSCS):
--[p157]-- During pre-alignment the mechanical correction accuracy is much less than measurement accuracy.
Correct for this by applying a wafer dependent Dynamic Wafer Offset (DWO), determined by the edge sensor or the mark sensor.

===== [p230-231] Alignment > Zero alignment: (3块) =====
--[p230]-- The information gives us the position and rotation of the reticle, with respect to the wafer stage.
Since we don’t expect to find large positioning errors for the reticle and stages after the zero alignment of the first wafer of the batch this scenario is then only done once per batch.
--[p231]-- When the option throughput enhancement is “off”, from the second wafer onward coarse alignment of the reticle will be skipped.
If throughput optimization is switched to “on” there will be no zero alignment performed from the second wafer from the batch onward.

By aligning on one of these marks, normally F2, the positions of reticle marks K1 and K2 are measured.

===== [p72-72] Step & Scan concepts > Step & Repeat vs Step & Scan: Slit Distortion Averaging (3块) =====
--[p72]-- These effects will be discussed later when we deal with imaging problems.
The picture on the left shows the distortion vectors (thin arrows) of the whole lens. The distortion is zero near the center, but gets gradually worse towards the edges. By only using the slit area, the distortion in the scan direction (Y) is almost nothing. The distortion in the non scanning direction (X) is still present.
--[p72]-- Step & Scan: lower lens distortion; both static and dynamic – Lens aberrations minimized over smaller (slit) field, – Averaging due to scanning: in scan direction distortion components average out better CD control.
The basic differences between a step & scan and a step & repeat system regarding distortion:
Because we only use a slit in the center of the lens during exposure, the influence of lens distortions appearing on the wafer can be made less than on a step & repeat system.
--[p72]-- The distortion in the non scanning direction (X) is still present.
Note that the distortion in the non scanning direction (X) will also be less because the lens could be optimized for a smaller area. (During manufacture, the lens elements are rotated with respect to each other to minimize the over all distortion.)

The Wafer Handling (WH) system will load, pre-align and unload wafers in the step & scan system.
Wafers can either enter the machine via a cassette or via a wafer coater/developer (also called track or wafer track). With a cassette a maximum of 25 wafers can be loaded into the machine. The track can just load 1 wafer at the time.
--[p102]-- The track can just load 1 wafer at the time.
The loading action is done by the Wafer Transport System (WTS).
To be able to make an exposure at the right XY position, we need to align the wafer to the reticle. This is done by the alignment system which will be discussed later on. To make sure that alignment can do a proper job, we need to pre-align the wafer, so it will be brought into the capture range of the alignment system. The pre-alignment action is done by the Wafer Pre-alignment System (WPS).
--[p105]-- This slide shows in more detail a library.
You can see the wafer detector with sender (laser source) and receiver. Also the positioning blocks for loading a cassette on the elevator plate can be seen.
--[p104]-- – End-effector robot puts wafer on pre-alignment unit (rotations unit moves up)
– When returning wafers to a cassette, the cassette is filled top-down.
To prevent contamination, the end effector always takes the lowest wafer out of a cassette. For the same reason, during unloading the wafers are put in a cassette starting at the top slot. This means that the input and output cassette can never be the same! This also means that the order of a set of wafers is reversed when they have been cycled through the machine once.
This slide shows in more detail a library.

Therefore this part is silent world.
The purpose of the Wafer Transport System (WTS) is to load wafers on the pre-alignment unit and to unload wafers from the discharge unit back to a cassette or pedestal. The WTS consists of 4 ports, which can have an elevator or a pedestal. The normal configuration is to have 2 elevators and 2 pedestals:
– elevator for wafer cassettes
– pedestal for wafer track interface (normally 1 cassette is used for the rejected wafers)

===== [p205-205] Alignment > Introduction: (2块) =====
--[p205]-- Prealignment takes place on the prealignment chuck.
After prealignment, the dipod transfers the wafer from the prealignment chuck to the exposure chuck in preparation for final alignment.
TTL- Alignment = Through The Lens Alignment
ATHENA = Advanced Technology using Higher order ENhanced Alignment
Athena will be covered in an other presentation.
Fast TTL = TTL with an improved algorithm, saving time, scans are queued
OA = Off axis alignment, this system uses higher orders which don’t travel through the lens.
--[p205]-- The function of the alignment system is to align the wafer to the reticle on the reticle table. The specification is critical because during the building of an IC up to 30 lithography steps might be required, necessity of good overlay between these steps (layers) is evident.
The alignment system has a capture range of +/- 44 m in the X and Y direction. To align successfully the wafer and reticle must first be positioned within this range. Prealignment takes place on the prealignment chuck.

===== [p351-351] CONTAMINATION & TEMPERATURE CONTROL (C&T) > C&T System Overview (5块) =====
--[p351]-- • The Air Control (AC) cabinet contains the fan unit to provide clean air, the Air control unit (ACU), and the air exhaustion fan to exhaust hot air generated by components and electronics of the Step & Scan system.
• The Charcoal Filter is placed before the Primary heat exchanger.
--[p351]-- • The C&T module is at the rear of the exposure unit. The C&T module filters the air using High Efficiency PArticles (HEPA) filters, and stabilizes the air temperature using heat exchangers. The C&T module also contains sensors for measuring air pressure, air flow, and restrictions for controlling the air flow.
• Manifolds are used to distribute air or water to the subsystems. Each subsystem (except the C&T module) is connected to the manifold using a ball valve.
--[p351]-- Each subsystem (except the C&T module) is connected to the manifold using a ball valve.
The actual temperature of the incoming water is measured at the manifold ( Twater in the C&T logfile ).
• AERIAL Air Box (AAB)
• Gas Control Unit (GCU) is used in Lens Purging to control the gas pressure and flow.
• Nitrogen Purging Unit (NPU)
--[p351]-- • The Charcoal Filter is placed before the Primary heat exchanger.
The Charcoal Filter removes (chemical) contaminants that exist in a wafer fab environment such as amines, ammonia (NH3), etc. Photoresists for DUV systems are sensitive to these contaminants so they cause damage to the photoresist on the wafer.
• The Primary heat exchanger is located at the rear of the exposure unit. Its function is to create sufficient temperature controlled air to the WTS and to lower the temperature of the air supplied to C&T module.
--[p351]-- • The Supply & Temperature (S&T) cabinet consists of the Main Switch Unit (MSU), the Temperature Control Unit (TCU), the TCU controller, the air extraction fan to remove particles from several subsystems of the Step & Scan system, the illumination power supply (on a /400 only), and the frequency controller which is used to change the speed of the water pump in the TCU.

===== [p263-264] Hardware overview > Levelling with Off axis (3块) =====
--[p264]-- In principle the Global Leveling (GL) information is used for leveling during OA align.
The variation in height and tilt is estimated to be smaller than 5 mm and 100 mrad. This is acceptable for alignment, so time can be saved, using the GL data instead of measuring per mark.
Since the focus/ tilt budget for processed marks can be more strict, the 5 mm and 100 mrad may no longer be acceptable. This would lead to a reduced reproducibility/ accuracy on these marks. In these cases it will help to switch “Level per mark” ON.
--[p264]-- \- Measurements per series of alignments (conform FTTL)
\- Level per mark optional (default NO)
– NO height and tilt are taken from Global Level
– YES All\_by\_LS measurement per mark
In the machine constants of Leveling Control, the option “level per mark” can be switched on. The other option for leveling is supposed to be for TTL, however the value of this switch is never used !
In principle the Global Leveling (GL) information is used for leveling during OA align.

===== [p188-189] LEVEL SENSOR (LS) FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Global level optimisation Y/N (5块) =====
--[p189]-- Ultra flat E-table: The new E-table is 8” only, one side usable and is restricted to just notch or flat edge type. This improves the clamping of the wafer near the edge, making it more flat.
2. Stone map: Makes a topography map of the wafer stage stone.
3. Several level control changes:
a. Change criterion to use a field as reference field for edge fields. Result is that the extrapolation distance decreases and thus the accuracy increases.
--[p189]-- Tilt to tilt matching: Tilt to tilt matching determines stone to lens tilt.
Currently this test is too inaccurate, the new test has high accuracy.

--[p188]-- To optimize the throughput it is possible to change the amount of global leveling. Normally every wafer that is loaded onto the wafer stage is globally leveled. By changing a machine constant this can be changed. The machine constant can be found in Machine Constants / Exposure Control / Global Level Optimisation. If this machine constant is set to yes, the global level sequence is changed. On the first wafer of a batch the normal global level action is performed (measuring 3 points).
--[p188]-- On the first wafer of a batch the normal global level action is performed (measuring 3 points).
For the other wafers of this batch the tilt of the first wafer is feed forwarded to the other wafers. To make sure the wafer surface is in the capture range of the white level sensor, one measurement in the center of the wafer is performed. This measurement is done with the red system. This sequence is also known as soft global level.
Engineering improvements to edge field leveling have been a high priority. Improvements which have been implemented are:
1.
--[p189]-- Result is that the extrapolation distance decreases and thus the accuracy increases.
Before a reference field had to be an inner field, now an intra field can be used as a reference field.
b. Implement critical percentage for better determination of the leveling modes of level sensor.
c. Shorten minimum All\_By\_LS area for intra field from 4mm to 1mm. Effectively reference information for edge fields will be taken closer to the edge field itself, giving more extrapolation accuracy.
4. Tilt to tilt matching: Tilt to tilt matching determines stone to lens tilt.

===== [p65-66] Machine Access FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Service: Swing Out Parts of Machine (4块) =====
--[p65]--  Swinging out parts of machine increases ease of service.
The above example of the three defined wafer stage configurations was added to this applications’ presentation to demonstrate that performing service on the Step & Scan system may well influence the metrology state of the system.
‘Metrology’ as an ASML term basically implies all that is needed to integrate the subsystems and make the Step & Scan system perform well. Most of it is software based.
--[p66]-- Please refer to week 2 of this course for more information.
The following parts of some subsystems can be swung out to improve accessibility to those parts and to the interior of the machine:
\- Granite stone
\- Advanced reticle management system
\- Objective lens (part of the illuminator)
\- Wafer transport system
\- Electronics cabinet
To further ease service to the machine some parts are remote, for example:
\- Laser (in case of a Deep UV system)
\- Supply and temperature control cabinet
\- Air control cabinet
\- Operator control unit
--[p65]-- Most of it is software based.
Swinging out the stone for service moves the stone from the position where it was calibrated during machine set-up. It will have to be swung back before the machine can operate again.
In practise it is impossible to precisely reproduce the location of the stone. Its orientation with respect to the projection lens will have changed. This makes swinging out the stone an applications issue.
--[p65]-- This makes swinging out the stone an applications issue.
When moving the stage over a tilted stone, the vertical stage position will vary with the horizontal stage position. In itself this is not bad. As long as we know how much the effect is, we can compensate for it. Hence we need to investigate it before using the machine for production again. In this case, re-calibrating the ‘stone\_to\_lens\_tilt’ will remedy the situation.
Please refer to week 2 of this course for more information.

===== [p125-126] Stage Movement Design > Scanning Wafer Stage Motors (4块) =====
--[p126]-- Three sensors known as LVDTs are also included in the exposure chuck.
These sensors are part of the leveling control system.
--[p125]-- The necessary power and pneumatic connections to the SWS are made with a defined dynamic short cut (see the Airmounts presentation).
The exposure chuck has several components. The wafer table (also known as the E-table or the pimple plate) is supported by the mirror block. The mirror block is separated from the airfoot by three cam discs. These are level actuators required for adjusting the height and tilt of the wafer. Three sensors known as LVDTs are also included in the exposure chuck.
--[p125]-- The scanning wafer stage (SWS) has two long stroke motors for movement in the X (stepping between exposures) and Y (scanning during exposures and stepping between exposures) directions. The X-motor is mounted on an external force frame. The Y-motor is mounted on top of the X-motor.
There are three SWS short stroke motors. Two provide movement along the Y axis and one along the X axis. This configuration allows for some rotation around the Z axis.
--[p125]-- This configuration allows for some rotation around the Z axis.
The coils for the short stroke motors are mounted on the dynamic body of the X long stroke motor. The permanent magnets are located inside the wafer stage (exposure chuck). As with the scanning reticle stage, the permanent magnets and the coils of the short stroke motors are not touching. This separation ensures that the SWS remains in the silent world despite being driven by motors that that are in the external world (the long stroke motors).

===== [p87-93] ADVANCED RETICLE MANAGEMENT SYSTEM (ARMS) > ASML CONFIDENTIAL (6块) =====
--[p93]-- Box free = The SMIF box on library 2 is free (not locked)
PPD = the IRIS system (see p. 11)
This slide shows some detail of the ARMS gripper (different for an ARMS gripper on a stepper). Note the Gpins, plunger, mechanical safety and how the reticle is supported in the gripper. There are 4 supporting points in Z, to prevent the reticle from falling down. Two of these points are the retractable G-pins. There are 3 supporting points in XY to prevent movement of the reticle during movement. One of these points is the retractable plunger.
--[p91]-- They will all measure the same intensity.
By comparing the signals of the 2 quadcells simultaneously, we can measure reticle rotation, reticle translation X and Y. The gripper will then move the reticle in X, Y and Rz until the quadcells measure the same signal.
This process of pre-alignment is first done at the coarse pre-alignment position and then repeated at the fine pre-alignment position. Note that since these 2 systems consist of same parts, the accuracy of both prealignment actions is the same (± 3 µm).
--[p93]-- This slide shows a special screen from the PAS software, obtained via the test manager. This slide displays the positions for the 2 grippers and the libraries. Note that both grippers are moving counter clockwise.
UP = the upper gripper. No reticle is loaded, indicated by the blue color (Park Pos.)
LO = the lower gripper. A reticle is loaded, indicated by the red color (Index2)
Box free = The SMIF box on library 2 is free (not locked)
Box locked = the SMIF box on library 1 is locked
--[p87]-- 1st version: 07-12-1999
2nd version: 15-12-1999
3th version: 03-04-2000
4th version 07-04-2000
5th version 13-04-2000
--[p91]-- The reticle needs to be pre-aligned to bring it in the center of the capture range of our alignment system. To be able to do this we need a reticle pre-alignment system (RPAS) and reticle pre-alignment marks. The reticle prealignment system consists of 2 LED’s and 2 quadcells. A quadcell is a photocell with 4 separate segments, each able to measure an intensity. The LED will illuminate the quadcell when the reticle pre-alignment marks put between these two.
--[p91]-- The LED will illuminate the quadcell when the reticle pre-alignment marks put between these two.
If there would be no mark, all 4 segments of the quadcell would measure the same intensity. Now that the reticle pre-alignment mark is in between the LED and the quadcell, the position of the reticle pre-alignment mark will influence the measured intensities. If the mark is positioned exactly in the center with respect to the quadcell, each of the 4 segments would suffer from the same intensity decrease. They will all measure the same intensity.

--[p494]-- The FEM on a scanner is very different from the steppers.
The purpose is still to find the best focus and energy within a matrix, but it is now used in a lot more ways.
On the steppers, the FEM is used as a tool to check for changes in focus at wafer level (best focus, LS height and tilt, etc).
With the scanner, this can also be true, but the FEM is now also used during system setup as a means of measuring the image plane both above, and below the lens.
Firstly, the scanner has a scanning reticle stage.
--[p492]-- The specification for accuracy appears high (3%).
According to customers this is acceptable, because it is less critical for them. It is just used to bring in the process window, so they can optimize it themselves.
Also be aware that we specify for the /500 repeatability and accuracy separately and not the sum of the two, like all the other PAS 5500 steppers.
Compare /300 ATP spec 1.5 % sum with /500 3.7% sum!
The FEM on a scanner is very different from the steppers.

– Accuracy [ % ] = abs | E set - E mean | / ( E set ) x 100%
The ATP data is based on the worst case dose accuracy and repeatability measurement during
spec accuracy = 3.0% spec repeatability = 0.7%
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
From the raw data the following performance indicators are calculated :
– - Repeatability [%]
– - Accuracy [%]
– - Sum = Repeatability + Accuracy [%]
Repeatability and Accuracy are defined for each dose as shown.
--[p492]-- Repeatability and Accuracy are defined for each dose as shown.
E\_set is the requested energy. E\_mean is the average of the 100 scan measurements for each dose. E\_max or E\_min are the maximum or minimum measurements from the 100 scan measurements.
The result to compare with the specification is the worst result over all the doses. This is specified in the report below the table results.
The Dose Repeatability has the tighter specification since it is more critical for production.
The specification for accuracy appears high (3%).

===== [p99-99] ADVANCED RETICLE MANAGEMENT SYSTEM (ARMS) > IRIS Principle (2块) =====
--[p99]-- Detection of particles on glass and pellicle side of the reticle by a scanning laser beam and detection of scattered light.
The IRIS detection principle is shown here. In case of a particle, scattered light is detected. The amount of scattered light is a measure of the size of the particle. Both top side and bottom side of a reticle can be inspected. However for the bottom side, a pellicle has to be mounted because the chrome pattern would disturb the measurement.
--[p99]-- So only the glass side and the pellicle side can be inspected. The top side and bottom side scan can not be done simultaneously. The scans would interfere with each other due to transmission and internal reflection in the reticle.

===== [p213-213] Alignment > Magnification: (1块) =====
--[p213]-- At reticle level the periods of the two gratings are 32 µm, and 35.2 µm respectively. However they are always referred to as 8.0 and 8.8 µm, to keep it similar to the line widths at wafer level. (see slide)

===== [p335-335] O > The mechanical aperture (3块) =====
--[p335]-- The mechanical aperture, also called the Numerical Aperture (NA,) is servo controlled, and is used to adjust the effective diameter of the projection lens, thus providing greater control of the projected reticle image. The mechanical aperture works like a diaphragm in a camera.
The DOF can also be influenced by changing the diameter of the NA: the smaller the NA diameter, the larger the DOF.
--[p335]-- The projection lens housing incorporates a cooling water coil that allows the temperature of the lens to be maintained at a constant 22-C.
A barometer measures the barometric pressure and a sensor measures the temperature of the lens.
Focus offsets that are required as a result of changes in the barometric pressure are compensated for by changing the laser beam wavelength. For the /400 the wavelength of the exposure light source can not be changed.
--[p335]-- For the /400 the wavelength of the exposure light source can not be changed.
Therefore on a /400 barometric pressure correction is applied in a different way. The lens elements are adjusted to compensate for any introduced error.

===== [p444-451] Image Quality? > Rotation (reticle/reticle image) (7块) =====
--[p451]-- Dynamic mode therefore shows the same error as static mode.
However, when the level sensor is tilted in the Y axis ( rotation around the X axis ), then there is no visible effect at the slit, so there is no effect on our image.
( In fact there will be a small focus difference between the edges of the slit, but the effect is negligible )
Dynamic IQ 48142 v00, 16/03/0 15
--[p446]-- Ideally, the image plane should be parallel to the lens.
If the image plane is tilted, due to the construction of the lens, then the focal plane will be equally tilted.
This is not a problem (within limits), as long as we know what the tilt is, so the reticle and wafer can be tilted by the same amount
If the reticle is not correctly adjusted, it will cause image problems on the wafer, which are quite different from those of the stepper.
Next slides will discuss these problems.
--[p448]-- In this slide, the rotation of the reticle table is in Y ( rotation around the X axis ).
Because our slit is along the center of the reticle, which is also the center of rotation of the reticle table, we do not see any error.
During a scanning exposure, as the reticle moves, every point of the reticle passes through the center of the lens and reticle table, where there is no tilt error. This means that the image on the wafer contains no error
--[p449]-- This slide is a 3 dimensional drawing of field curvature , caused by the z manipulator lenses. Over the whole lens area the effect is in both X and Y directions, but after scanning through our infinitely small slit, the remaining effect is only in the X direction.
This slide deals with image tilt, caused by a tilt in the level sensor. In this case, the tilt is in the X axis ( rotation around the Y axis ). Dynamic mode therefore shows the same error as static mode.
--[p447]-- Next slides will discuss these problems.
Reticle table tilt causes trapezoid errors. The left picture shows the effect over the whole lens area ( static mode )
Again, using our infinitely small slit, the effect of reticle table tilt is seen only in the X direction ( rotation around the Y axis.)
Only the distortion which is in the slit has any influence on the final image.
Notice in this case, that there is no distortion in the center of the image along the Y axis, as this is the center of rotation of the reticle table

One important new aspect to consider is that the reticle stage is no longer static. This means that the height and tilt can change during a scan.
Therefore, we have to control the reticle stage to make sure that it runs parallel to the image plane of the lens.

Observe that the scanned picture is rotated like on a stepper.

===== [p206-206] Alignment > Reticle pre-alignment: (2块) =====
--[p206]-- Before aligning the wafer to the reticle, they must both be loaded within the capture range of the alignment system.
This requires pre-alignment of both the reticle and the wafer before they are placed on their respective stages.
Reticle pre-alignment consists of coarse and fine pre-align.
The coarse reticle pre-alignment is done on the ARMS (Advanced Reticle Management System) gripper using the same method as the fine pre-alignment.
--[p206]-- Fine reticle pre-alignment is done directly above the reticle stage.
There are two reticle pre-alignment marks on the reticle.
There are quadcell detectors on the top of the lens and LEDs on the reticle handling unit.
Pre-alignment takes place by positioning the reticle so that the signals on all four quadrants of both detectors are equal.
The accuracy of reticle pre-alignment is app.. 3 µm.

===== [p161-163] Stage Movement Design > Right: (6块) =====
--[p161]-- To make sure where it is and at what angle, we must perform what is called a stage to stage align.
This stage to stage align gives us more data that we need, as is explained on the next slide.
Note that on the middle and right pictures, translation is not shown. This was done to keep the pictures as clear as possible.
During alignment, the interferometer data from the reticle and wafer stage are compared, and appropriate positions are calculated.
If we are to perform a decent scan, the wafer should move in the same direction as the reticle image.
--[p163]-- This slide displays the relevant stage coordinate systems.
The reticle coordinate system and reticle stage coordinate system with their positive X- and Y-axis can be seen. Furthermore the wafer coordinate system and wafer stage coordinate system are shown. It can be seen that the positive X- and Y-axis are opposite on wafer and reticle stage. The wafer stage is the holy coordinate system. This means that all parameters are calculated into wafer stage coordinates.
All scanners have a projection lens with a reduction factor of 4.
--[p161]-- To move the reticle stage, we need to keep track of where it is. For this we use the HP interferometer. It defines the coordinate system in which the stage moves: the Reticle Stage Coordinate System (RSCS). Since the reticle stage can float and rotate freely on top of the stone, it could be at any angle and position with respect to this coordinate system. To make sure where it is and at what angle, we must perform what is called a stage to stage align.
--[p163]-- This will introduce an error called scan scaling.
These 2 errors are displayed in the dotted circle on the slide. $\vee _ { 0 , \forall + } ,$ is the expected (correct) wafer stage scan length and direction. $\mathsf { V } _ { 0 , \mathsf { y } + }$ is the real wafer stage scan length and direction. The 2 errors, skew and scaling can be seen. The effects of both errors are explained in the dynamic image quality presentation.
--[p162]-- Which means that we should express the Reticle Stage Coordinate System in terms of the Wafer Stage Coordinate System. This, plus finding reticle stage rotation, is determined during a stage to stage align.
When the RSCS has been related to the WSCS, the wafer can be moved underneath the lens, at the same relative position, at the same relative speed and along the same axis as the reticle image above the lens.
This slide displays the relevant stage coordinate systems.
--[p163]-- All scanners have a projection lens with a reduction factor of 4.
This means that the scanning reticle stage has to move exactly 4 times a longer distance than the scanning wafer stage, and in the opposite direction as well. From these 2 conditions, 2 errors can occur. First of all the scan direction of the 2 stages with respect to each other can be wrong. This will introduce an error called scan skew. Besides that the scan length of the 2 stages with respect to each other can be wrong. This will introduce an error called scan scaling.

===== [p117-117] Stage Movement Design (3块) =====
--[p117]-- As the name suggests, short stroke motors have a short range of travel of the order of a few mm. Long stroke motors have a relatively long range of travel, of the order of a few hundred mm. The former are Lorenz motors and the latter, linear motors. Both types of motors have a fixed and a moving body. The moving and static bodies do not touch. This is a primary reason for choosing these motors.
--[p117]-- This is a primary reason for choosing these motors.
As there is no contact between the moving and stationary bodies, no vibrations are transmitted through the motors to the stages.
The slide shows a short stroke motor stator fixed to the moving body of the a long stroke motor. What is not shown is a sensor that monitors the relative positions of the short stroke motor bodies. Any displacement results in a signal that causes the long stroke motor to move. Thus the long stroke motor tries to keep up with the short stroke motor.
--[p117]-- Thus the long stroke motor tries to keep up with the short stroke motor.
This is the master and slave principle.
Note that both stages move over large pieces of granite known as “stones” with minimal friction. This will be discussed further in some of the coming slides.

===== [p14-15] Wavelength vs. Depth of Focus (2) > Wavelength vs. Depth of Focus (3) (8块) =====
--[p14]-- In both cases, the process resolves the same minimum image size.
However in the bottom example the distance from the exact point of recombination of orders (the focal plane) is greater than in the top example simply because the angle of diffraction is not as large. Therefore, the bottom example allows a greater tolerance in focus before the ability to resolve this minimum image size is lost.
This illustration helps to show the effect of Numerical Aperture with highly collimated illumination and a slightly more complex lens.
--[p15]-- The drawing on the right makes two statements about properties of the optical system. The circle is meant to define the entrance pupil of the lens as well as the position of the aperture. This entrance pupil is the plane at the point in the optical system where order information from all points of the image cross over itself, as seen in the left drawing. The three spots within the circle are the -1st, 0th, and +1st orders of the light collected from a slit at the reticle.
--[p15]-- So we can see from this that for a given wavelength of light, and a given lens NA, features whose 1st order diffraction angle falls within the diameter of the aperture will be resolved by the lens. Notice that the value of NA has essentially no effect on the 0th order information. It is this relationship that defines traditional minimum resolution for an optical system.
We also see from this drawing how a lens inverts the image of the reticle.
--[p14]-- In this example, the vertical parallel lines define the ability of the process to resolve an image. The top drawing shows the orders recombining at a wide angle resulting from illumination at a longer wavelength, for example 248-nm.
The bottom drawing shows the orders from the same feature recombining at a narrower angle resulting from illumination at a shorter wavelength, for example 193- nm.
In both cases, the process resolves the same minimum image size.
--[p14]-- That range (in Z) is the range of focus, or the “Depth of Focus.”
Optical Theory 48142 v00, 12-07-2000 10
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
For a fixed reticle image size, this slide illustrates the effect on depth of focus. Critical to this entire issue of depth of focus is the resist process itself. Materials, pre- and postexposure processing (the “process”) will all determine the ability of the system to resolve a feature over some depth of focus.
--[p15]-- Notice in the illustration that the first order information from the reticle images just passes within the limit established by the NA setting. NA is defined as the sine of the maximum diffraction angle which can pass through the lens. The diffraction angle is based upon the size of the image and the wavelength of illumination. So if the NA of the lens is smaller than this trigonometric relationship, the image cannot be resolved by the lens.
--[p14]-- When the reticle image is constant, the angle of the diffracted orders will be greater if the wavelength is longer, i.e. 248 than if the wavelength is shorter , i.e. 193.
When the components (images carried by the orders) are reassembled in the same relationship they were when they were at the reticle, they will be in focus.
Within a few nanometers, these components will be close enough together (in X and Y) to form an acceptable image.
--[p15]-- The size of the spots are the result of how well collimated the illumination is: that is, the more collimated the illumination, the smaller will be the spots. (We are speaking here of directional collimation, or how much the light diverges as distance from the source increases.)
The diameter of the circle is the numerical aperture of the lens. The position of the -1st and +1st order information is based upon the diffraction angle of the 1st order light through the slit.

===== [p455-458] Image Quality? > Reticle Stage Wedge (5块) =====
--[p457]-- Distortions can be caused by a number of things, the most important being:
There may be distortion in the lens,
and the machine may not be properly setup in X,Y, and Z . e.g. RB offsets not calibrated
This averaging out of the error is called fading.
It can be seen like so:
As the pattern is exposed through the slit, the exposure will start at the bottom of the slit, which in this example has a (Lens) mag. error in X and Y, which can be drawn as X and Y vectors.
--[p457]-- In reality, this is not so.
Slit size =26 x 11mm
This means that if there is some distortion in the slit area, the total effect will be an average of all errors as the wafer and reticle scan over the slit.
In other words, the direction of the distortion may change over the height of the slit, causing a change in distortion on any one point of the pattern as it travels through the slit.
Distortions can be caused by a number of things, the most important being:
The reticle and wafer stages are not properly synchronized,
--[p458]-- error in X and Y, which can be drawn as X and Y vectors.
As the same point on the wafer scans through the slit, it will pass through areas where the error vectors are different, finally passing through the top of the slit, where the Y vector is the opposite of the start of the slit.
This means that the image will appear to be ‘smeared’ in the Y direction.
The error in the X axis goes from a large error at the bottom of the slit, through a smaller error at the middle, back to a large error at the top.
This means that the image will also be smeared in the X axis, but not as much as in Y It is this ‘smearing’ that defines the Fading of the image.
--[p456]-- Scan Rotation involves rotation of the Reticle Stage during the scanning process.
It is caused by bad calibration of the Interferometer beams, which measure the position in Y-direction, if both beams are not perfectly parallel. Therefore, during scanning the Reticle Stage is being rotated slightly into the Y-direction. Basically, Scan Rotation is dealt with by calibrating the Reticle Stage Interferometers during factory Set-up.

===== [p341-341] Introduction to semiconductor industry FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Means of area selection: Lithography (3块) =====
--[p341]-- – A photo-active coating (photoresist) is put on the wafer surface.
– The path of the light towards the wafer surface is partially blocked by the patterns on a mask (reticle). Part of the coating is exposed.
– The exposed part is chemically changed by the light.
– Washing away the changed (or unchanged) parts yields a partially blocked area on the wafer surface.
– Process steps can now be performed on the selected area.
--[p341]-- – Process steps can now be performed on the selected area.
Lithography is better and cheaper than other techniques that involve physical covering or scratching.
The result of having washed away the changed (or unchanged) parts of the photo-active coating is that part of the wafer is covered with the coating and part of it is not. Hence part of the wafer will be blocked because there is a coating on it, the other part will not be blocked. Thus the effect of the next process step will be localized on the unblocked parts of the wafer.
--[p341]-- Thus the effect of the next process step will be localized on the unblocked parts of the wafer.
After this process step, the resist can be removed. The sequence is repeated (each time with different process steps, of course) until the chips are finished.

===== [p330-335] O > The beam dump (12块) =====
--[p333]-- This filter is designed for a specific machine which has stable hot and/or cold spots.
At the location of a hot spot, the transmission of the filter is less, thus compensating the hot spot and improving the uniformity of the illumination field.
The main function of the projection lens is to reduce (four times) and project the reticle image onto a wafer.
The projection lens is a double telecentric lens that reduces the image on the reticle four times. A double telecentric lens is a lens whose magnification does not vary by moving the reticle or the wafer in Z.
--[p333]-- This lens assembly is mounted on a supporting column and can be rotated away from the reticle table when being serviced. The REMA lens also comprises the condenser lens.
The reticle masking lens projects the REMA blade plane onto the reticle.
The plane of the condenser lens can be adjusted to achieve best uniformity at reticle level by correcting any mechanical tilts of the light source..
--[p330]-- The beam dump is mounted in the horizontal plane behind the energy sensor. It absorbs light that is reflected back from the REMA blades. This light needs to be absorbed to ensure dose accuracy.
Purpose of the integrator is to make the illumination field as even as possible. Historically a fly’s eye lens was used (an optical element with 112 micro-lenses over its surface). These 112 lenses acted as individual sources, all of which were integrated into a single illumination field.
--[p335]-- The two lens groups, called Z 1 and Z 2, are driven by a servo motor with an encoder.
Z 1, also called Z-actuator 1, can be moved up or down to minimise image field curvature (focal plane curvature at wafer level). Z 2, also called Z-actuator 2, can be moved up or down to correct lens magnification and wafer expansion. Actually, when either group is adjusted, the other group is moved in a counter direction to compensate, the reticle plane is adjusted and focus may also be adjusted.
Correction of magnification and/or field curvature is a complex task, involving all these parameters.
--[p332]-- The reticle masking blade setting is projected onto the reticle by the reticle masking objective.
Because the reticle masking blades are positioned at a small distance from the exit end of the quartz rod, a shadow from the REMA blade edges will appear on the reticle, causing the half shadow effect. The half shadow causes a partly illuminated edge around the area on the reticle that was intended to be exposed..
--[p331]-- This function is now accomplished through use of a quartz rod (actually two rods with AERIAL and one with AERIAL-II) with highly parallel sides which serves to integrate more than 900 full field images of the illumination source at the REMA objective.
--[p332]-- The user must ensure that the chrome border surrounding the image on the reticle is wide enough that the cells adjacent to the one being exposed on the wafer will not be exposed accidentally. These specifications are included in the reticle manual, and is supplied to all commercial mask vendors as well as the customer.
The reticle masking window setting is projected onto the reticle by means of the reticle masking objective (also called REMA lens).
--[p333]-- A gradient filter can be attached to the bottom of the condenser lens to increase the uniformity of the illumination field by compensating variations in the illumination system intensity. The purpose of the gradient (or: gray) filter is to remove hot or cold spots from the uniformity profile. This filter is designed for a specific machine which has stable hot and/or cold spots.
--[p331]-- The AERIAL optics precede the quartz rod, so any collimation setting at the AERIAL module (sigma and/or annular settings) are duplicated at the output of the quartz rod.
REMA stands for REticle MAsking. The REMA unit consists of four blades that mask the unused part of the reticle during scanning. Each REMA blade is driven by a servo (stepper) motor and can move to any coordinates within the illumination field to isolate any part of the reticle image.
--[p332]-- The REMA unit makes it possible to expose only a single section of the reticle. The REMA blades can be set, as shown on the slide, to expose any rectangular or square area by masking the remainder of the reticle.
The reticle masking blade setting is projected onto the reticle by the reticle masking objective.
--[p331]-- Because the center of each entrance illumination field is in a different location than any other, bright or dark spots within the source field are integrated over the entire output, thus providing extremely uniform illumination to the REMA objective.
It is important to note that the directional collimation characteristics of the illumination entering the quartz rod are duplicated at its output.
--[p334]-- The projection lens consists of the following main components:
– Two movable lens groups,
– A mechanical aperture.
– About 20 other elements
– An alignment prism needed for the alignment system
The major components of the projection lens of the Step & Scan system are shown on the slide. The movable lens groups
The two lens groups, called Z 1 and Z 2, are driven by a servo motor with an encoder.

===== [p69-71] Step & Scan concepts > Note: (8块) =====
--[p69]-- The scan directions of wafer and reticle are opposite. This because the lens inverts the image (point symmetry of the lens).
The masking blades are set to a slit, instead of the full field as with a stepper. The reason for this is explained later in this presentation.
The basic differences between a step & scan and a step & repeat system regarding field size:
--[p71]-- \- The upper bucket represents the light source, and the sand is the energy dose.
\- For exposure, the bottom of the bucket opens, releasing the sand, which fills the lower bucket uniformly (assuming the shutter opens instantaneously).
\- After the required sand level (dose) has been obtained, the bottom closes immediately.
Important in this respect is that the whole area of the die becomes exposed simultaneously and that during exposure the die remains stationary.
The ‘slit sized hour glass’ model illustrates a step & scan exposure:
--[p70]-- i.e. the image field can be bigger than on a step & scan system with the same size lens.
Note that in the none scan-direction (X), the size is limited by the lens, while in the scan-direction (Y), die size is limited only by the movement range of the stages that support reticle and wafer.
The best part of a lens is in the center. A step & repeat system uses the whole lens area to expose, but a step & scan system uses only a ‘slit’ in the center of the lens.
--[p71]-- Thus, Dose Control and Dose Uniformity are both determined by stage-to-laser synchronization.
--[p71]-- \- The unexposed die is represented by a bucket that needs to be filled with sand.
\- The light source is represented by an hour glass that scans the bucket below.
\- The exit of the hour glass has the width of the illuminating slit at wafer level.
\- It delivers sand in pulses, represented here by a shutter that quickly opens and closes, simulating a laser firing into the illuminating slit during exposure.
\- While the bucket is being scanned under the slit, the sand fills up the bucket.
--[p70]-- This means that in the scan direction only the center of the lens is used, therefore causing less distortion.
The basic differences between a step & scan and a step & repeat system regarding exposure dose control:
The ’full-field bucket model' illustrates the exposure procedure in a step & repeat system:
\- The lower bucket represents the field on the wafer that is to be exposed.
\- The upper bucket represents the light source, and the sand is the energy dose.
--[p70]-- A step & repeat system exposes a pattern onto a wafer by exposing the whole image field at once, so the maximum field size is limited to the size of the projection lens, and vice-versa.
A step & scan system exposes the pattern by moving the reticle across the lens, while at the same time moving the wafer across the lens.
Advantage to this is that the reticle and the field size is no longer controlled by the field size of the lens.
--[p71]-- \- While the bucket is being scanned under the slit, the sand fills up the bucket.
Scanning too quickly results in an insufficient amount (dose) in the bucket. Scanning too slowly delivers too large an amount (dose). Therefore, scanning at a non-uniform scanning speed results in non-uniform dose distribution.
This is represented by the small bump of sand in the two pictures on the right. The bump suggests that the bucket was scanned momentarily with too low a speed, resulting in one ’pulse' of sand too many delivered at that position.

===== [p182-187] LEVEL SENSOR (LS) FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Note: (12块) =====
--[p186]-- This sensitivity is expressed in a percentage and is called the critical percentage.
With this critical percentage you can change the amount of Alll\_By\_LS, Z\_By\_LS and None\_By\_LS area on the wafer. The critical percentage can be set between 66 and 100 %. A low critical percentage will give an increase in measured area by the level sensor, but a decrease in accuracy.
For a stepper the above story is correct. For a scanner however up to and including software release 8.3, the critical percentage is fixed to 87 %.

\- Factory Constants / Job Definition Data / Preview Focus Mode / Critical Percentage - Machine Constants / Leveling Control / Flexible Level Spot Selection / Critical Percentage (only for release 8.3 and below)

Focus edge clearance:

--[p187]-- Therefore we can specify an area in which the level sensor is not allowed to perform any measurements. This focus edge clearance specifies the invalid area from the wafer edge where the level sensor can not be used for measurements. The focus edge clearance is measured from the wafer edge in mm. Usually the focus edge clearance is larger than the round and flat edge clearance. The focus edge clearance can be set from 0 to 100 mm, but it is more likely to have a value around 5 mm.
--[p185]-- The critical percentage in the machine constants and the focus edge clearance in the Factory Constants / Batch control data, were the values that were actually used during batch control (exposing a job). With the introduction of the optimized scanner route, the function of these parameters changed. The changes will be explained on the next slides. The effects will be explained in the presentation about scanner routing.
--[p186]-- The critical percentage is only a ‘tool’ in the software.
E.g. a critical percentage of 66% doesn’t mean that the light spots are 66% of the normal size. It only means that if the part of the spot that is within the Focus Edge Clearance represents 66% (or more) of the total sensitivity, it will be used as a valid spot.
Due to wafer/resist unflatness at the wafer edge and due to edge bead removal, we don’t want to use the most outside part of the wafer for our leveling measurements.
--[p184]-- The level sensor has a huge impact on the throughput on a scanner. The throughput on a scanner can be optimized by changing the routing (exposure sequence) of a job. This is an automated function which is explained in a separate presentation on routing.
There are some parameters that will influence the leveling on a scanner. They will be discussed on the next slides.
Note:
The critical percentage and focus edge clearance both can be found on different positions in the PAS software.
--[p186]-- This is the area specified by the 5 intersection points (indicated by the 5 red spots) of the level sensor spot with an imaginary slit of 8x26 mm. So neither the value from Factory Constants / Job Definition Data / Preview Focus Mode / Focus Edge Clearance nor the one from Machine Constants / Leveling Control / Flexible Level Spot Selection / Critical Percentage is used. That means that a change in these factory and machine constant have NO effect.
--[p185]-- Focus edge clearance:
\- Factory Constants / Batch Control Data / Focus Edge Clearance
The values for critical percentage and focus edge clearance in the Factory Constants / Job definition data were originally meant for viewing purposes. A customer would be able to see the impact of a change on a job, without affecting the existing jobs (just a preview during creation of a job).
--[p186]-- That means that a change in these factory and machine constant have NO effect.
From software release 8.4 and up, the critical percentage will be introduced as an extra parameter in job definition and batch control. This parameter will influence the level sensor functionality and therefor the routing. So from this release it is possible to change the critical percentage similar as with steppers.
Note: The physical size of the four light spots remains the same! The critical percentage is only a ‘tool’ in the software.
--[p187]-- The level sensor functionality (All\_By\_LS, Z\_By\_LS and None\_By\_LS) is dependent by this focus edge clearance.
For the level sensor the wafer radius is:
r = Wd/2 -FEC
Where:
r: radius
Wd: Wafer diameter, as specified in the Factory Constants/Job Definition Data / Wafer Layout / Diameter
FEC: Focus Edge Clearance, as specified in Factory Constants/Batch Control Data / Focus Edge Clearance
--[p182]-- If for an inter field no reference field is available, a measurement scan is performed beside the field to be exposed to determine the required parameter(s).
--[p186]-- The effects will be explained in the presentation about scanner routing.
The sensitivity for height variations is not equal for the whole level sensor spot. The middle of the spot is the most sensitive part and will therefore contribute the most to the height measurement. The most sensitive area must fall on the wafer within the focus edge clearance. The user can program a criterion: what percentage must be within the focus edge clearance for the spot to be considered valid. This sensitivity is expressed in a percentage and is called the critical percentage.

===== [p135-135] Stage Movement Design > Loading a Wafer (Interface with Wafer Pre-alignment System) (3块) =====
--[p135]-- The slide above illustrates that while moving to the wafer load position, the wafer stage Y mirror moves out of the path of the Y interferometer beams. Thus the stage ceases to be under the control of the Y interferometer. Therefore an alternative means of controlling the Y position of the stage is included. This is known as the load sensor. It consists of an array of magnets arranged on the mirror block (their poles lie along the Y axis) and a Hall sensor mounted on the sensor plate.
--[p135]-- As indicated in the slide, the load sensor takes control of the Y position when the stage reaches the takeover position. From here the load sensor maintains the Y position of the stage. The load position is reached under control of the X interferometer.
The precision of the Load sensor is orders of magnitude less than the interferometers. The former controls the stage position to within a few microns, the latter to within less than a nanometer.
--[p135]-- This is sufficient to ensure that wafers will be brought within the capture range of the alignment system.
The position of the hall sensor is readily adjustable.
More information regarding wafer handling can be found in the Wafer handler presentation.

===== [p218-219] Alignment > Alignment mark gratings: (4块) =====
--[p219]-- If the wafer is shifted over n times 8 µm from its correct position, the 8 µm grating still shows zero error, but the 8.8 µm grating shows an error of 8 x (n/10) µm. Only if the displacement is 88 µm will a new aligned position be found.
With a combination of 8.0 µm and 8.8 µm the maximum intensity is repeated every 10 periods (for 8.8) giving the system a capture range of +/- 44 µm.
--[p218]-- As can be seen from this slide, a primary alignment mark contains four phase gratings. Two gratings are for alignment along the X axis (X8.0 and X8.8), and two are for the Y axis (Y8.0 and Y8.8). This slide shows a primary mark type PM.
Each grid is detected by one quadrant on the quadcell detector.
The wafer is perfectly aligned if the signals are evenly detected by each quadrant of the quadcell detector. This will be discussed in more detail later.
--[p219]-- To increase the capture range from +/- 4 µm to +/- 44 µm, a vernier system is used. To achieve this, there are two gratings for both X and Y alignment. One grating has the nominal grating period, and one is 10% wider than nominal.
The alignment system assumes that correct alignment has been achieved when both 8 µm and 8.8 µm gratings coincide with the reticle (that is, when the sine waves generated by the electronics coincide).
--[p219]-- This will be discussed in more detail later.
The alignment system determines the position where the reticle grating coincides with the wafer grating (in actual fact with the image of the wafer grating). This is done by scanning the image of the wafer mark over the reticle mark. If the images coincide, the detector measures the highest intensity.
With only an 8.0 µm grating the maximum intensity would occur every 8.0 µm, giving the system a capture range of +/- 4 µm.

===== [p132-132] Stage Movement Design > The Wafer Stage Interferometers (2块) =====
--[p132]-- The slide illustrates the configuration of the wafer stage interferometers. The illustration shows that both use three beams. On each mirror, two measurement beams are incident at the same height. The third hits the mirror below the others.
Having two measurement beams at the same height enables the interferometer system to measure stage rotation. Rotation can be determined by one interferometer. Indeed only the X interferometer is used for that purpose.
--[p132]-- Indeed only the X interferometer is used for that purpose.
The extra beam is included in the Y interferometer for use in tests. For example it is used in the determination of mirror unflatness. This test will be discussed during the practical part of the course.
The third beam is included to monitor mirror block tilt. Such tilt causes an X Y position error. This is known as the Abbe error. The interferometers are calibrated during a test to compensate for this tilt-induced positioning error.
This will also be discussed during the practical part of the course.

===== [p330-330] O > The energy sensor (1块) =====
--[p330]-- The energy sensor measures the light intensity during exposures.

===== [p74-76] FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Objectives (5块) =====
--[p75]-- The external world, which contains non-critical parts of the machine The silent world, containing the most critical parts of the machine.
Exposure begins when the reticle and wafer stage have accelerated to a synchronised scanning speed. The desired acceleration and large masses require considerable force and place extreme demands on system dynamic design and vibration isolation as explained in the previous slides.
--[p74]-- After completion of this module the student will be able to:
• State the functions of the airmounts sub-system.
• Identify the major parts of the step and scan system in the silent and external worlds
• Identify all actuators and sensors used on the airmounts system and state their function.
• Explain the term dynamic shortcut and its effects
