The above slide states the objectives of this training module.
--[p74]-- This sub-system will be discussed in this presentation.
1. The external world.
2. The silent or suspended world.
We need to keep the silent world isolated as much as possible from the external world.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
The efficiency and accuracy of the exposure process can be severely degraded by vibration of the wafer, the reticle or the lens. To guard against this the machine has been divided into 2, namely:
--[p76]-- In the above diagram, the illuminator, lens and parts of the wafer stage are isolated from the external world. Note parts of the reticle stage are isolated along with the alignment system, level sensor, image sensor and the wafer prealignment system. Scanning motor acceleration forces in the system are absorbed by the external force frame.
--[p74]-- The above slide states the objectives of this training module.
It is of great importance in wafer fabrication that the images defined in lithographic steps are sharp. ASML scanners therefore need to keep process critical areas of the machine vibration free, as even minute movement during exposure causes extreme degradation of the lithographic process. As it would be impossible to isolate the whole machine, a suspension system has been developed to stabilise the mainplate during the scanning action of the stages and to isolate vibrations from the fab floor.

===== [p453-454] Image Quality? > Scan Direction (Scan Skew) (3块) =====
--[p453]-- Scan skew causes asymmetric rotation
Now we will take a look at the effects that are specific to the scanner.
As briefly mentioned on an earlier slide, the scan direction of the reticle and wafer stages have to be synchronized. i.e., they have to scan parallel to each other, and at the same relative speed.
Any error in the scan direction will produce a skew error
When skew is seen on the image, it is impossible to tell whether it was caused by wafer or reticle stage problems.
--[p453]-- The effect of scan skew on the wafer is an asymmetric rotation error.
In this slide we see a dynamic error only in the Y axis.
Picture 1 shows the effect if the stages are not synchronized at the start of the scan
Picture 2 shows the effect in one scan direction if the stages are synchronized
Picture 3 shows the same effect for the opposite scan direction
Notice the direction of the error at the top and bottom of the image.
This effect is caused by a problem with the scan speed.
--[p454]-- This effect is caused by a problem with the scan speed.
During a scan the reticle and wafer stages must be synchronized to travel at the same relative
speed. If the speeds are not correct then this is the result. The image appears to
stretch/shrink, or ‘smear’, over the wafer.
this effect is called scan scaling.
The effect of scan scale on the wafer is an asymmetric magnification error.

===== [p238-239] Alignment > 8.0 to 8.8 shift: (5块) =====
--[p239]-- A delta shift can also be caused by changes in lens distortion characteristics, such as when third order distortion is adjusted.
Well defined marks, aligned on a calibrated scanner, will show delta shifts well below 0.1 µm.
This shift is bound between - 0.4 µm for the following reason.
The software always selects the two maxima that are closest together as being the best position. But if one signal is shifted more then 0.4 µm, it will select the wrong 8.0- maximum. ((8.8 - 8.0)/2 = 0.4)
--[p238]-- Due to the different paths, a shift between both 8.0 and 8.8 micron alignment signals is induced.
This difference is referred to as 8.0-8.8 shift as shown on this sheet.
This shift is measured during machine setup, during basic machine constants, with the WH-calibration wafer. The result is saved in the machine constants.
The software always selects the two maxima that are closest together as being the best position. But if one signal is shifted more then 0.4 µm, it will select the wrong 8.0- maximum.
--[p238]-- However, even at the actual position of the mark, the 8.0 and 8.8 transitions don’t exactly coincide. The different spatial frequency of the two gratings results in slightly different diffraction angles for the first order information, consequently the first orders light paths in the lens and all other optics for both 8.0 and 8.8 micron gratings are different.
Due to the different paths, a shift between both 8.0 and 8.8 micron alignment signals is induced.
--[p239]-- But if one signal is shifted more then 0.4 µm, it will select the wrong 8.0- maximum.
This error is not visible on the monitor of the operator console.
--[p239]-- But if one signal is shifted more then 0.4 µm, it will select the wrong 8.0- maximum.
Due to deformation or any rotation of the alignment mark, an additional shift between both curves is induced. Deformation can be caused by process, for instance chemical mechanical polishing. Mark rotations are caused by reticle pre-alignment inaccuracies.
The difference between this actual measured value and the machine constant, 8.0-8.8 shift, is called the delta 8.0-8.8 Shift as shown on this slide.

===== [p194-194] LEVEL SENSOR (LS) FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Scanner Routing Determination (3块) =====
--[p194]-- Once the optimized route has been calculated, this route is fixed for the job and all layers will be exposed using the same route. This is even machine independent. The reason to do this in job definition is that it can take some time to calculate this optimized route and that would slow down the start of a batch. The route calculation is done using a mathematical algorithm called simulated annealing. For every reticle field (which is an image) a new optimized route has to be calculated.
--[p194]-- For every reticle field (which is an image) a new optimized route has to be calculated.
Note: Use of the word ‘image’ is linked to the use in the PAS software. During Image Definition several images can be created on 1 or more reticles. An image is an area on a reticle that needs to be exposed. One reticle can contain several images which can be beside each other, overlapping or even on the same area of the reticle.
--[p194]--  Up to and including release 8.3 the optimized scanner route is determined in Job Definition and passed to Batch Control / Exposure Control.
 Why:
Route calculation takes up to 3 minutes per image (a reticle field ). So batch startup would be delayed because of this calculation (lower throughput).
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
Up to and including software release 8.3, the calculation of the optimized route can only be done in job definition.

===== [p146-146] Stage Movement Design > The Linear Variable Displacement Transducer (LVDT) (2块) =====
--[p146]-- Measure resonance frequency of coil as a function of vertical distance d.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
A Linear Variable Displacement Transducer measures a distance between two bodies.
Measurement principle:
On one body a coil is fixed. On the other, a metal rod is fixed that has a different conductivity for magnetic fields than the surrounding air.
When a current is applied to the coil, a magnetic field will be created.
--[p146]-- When a current is applied to the coil, a magnetic field will be created.
This effect will not be instantaneous, it will take a short while for the magnetic field to build up. The speed at which the field is generated depends greatly on the magnetic permeability of the materials surrounding the coil. By moving the metal rod closer to the coil, the speed at which a magnetic field builds up is changed.
The electronics attached to the coil measure the ‘reaction speed’ of the coil by measuring its resonance frequency.
Once the LVDT has been properly calibrated, the resonance frequency of the coil is a measure of the distance d.

===== [p192-192] LEVEL SENSOR (LS) FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Stepper Routing (2块) =====
--[p192]-- On steppers the route calculation is very simple.
First with a meandering movement the inner [dies] fields (All\_By\_LS) will be exposed. After that all edge [dies] fields (Z\_By\_LS and None\_By\_LS) will be exposed in a circular movement.
This routing is determined during job definition (using some parameters) and again during batch control (using possible different parameters). Calculating it doesn’t take much time and therefore is done in batch control using the different parameters (the correct ones).
The relevant parameters that will determine the route, will be discussed later in this presentation.
--[p192]--  Stepper
– The stepper route is determined during Job Definition (JD), only used for visualization purposes.
– The stepper route is not passed to Batch Control (BC).
– The stepper route is determined in Batch Control and passed to Exposure Control (EC).
– Reason: there is no timing issue and less data is passed from Job Definition to Batch Control.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
On steppers the route calculation is very simple.

===== [p352-352] CONTAMINATION & TEMPERATURE CONTROL (C&T) > Water System (1) (3块) =====
--[p352]-- Provides water for controlling the temperature of various parts of the machine, also used to control the air temperature of the clean air system (air showers).
 Temperature Control Unit (TCU)
• Water System : The Water System provides water for controlling the temperature of various parts of the machine. The Water is also used to control the air temperature of the air going to the airshowers by means of a heat exchanger.
--[p352]-- The water is cycled through the exposure unit in a closed loop system and its temperature is carefully controlled. • Temperature Control Unit (TCU) determines the water temperature in the Water System and makes sure the water circulates through the system. A TCU basically consists of a Water Tank equipped with a Heater and a Cooler Circuit.To prevent clogging and corrosion, ultra pure water is used in the water system.
--[p352]-- The PAS software determines the setpoint of the Temperature Control Unit (TCU), while the Temperature Control Unit (TCU) controller tries to stabilize the water temperature at this setpoint. • Bypass valve is needed to adjust the water flow to the exposure unit.

--[p327]-- Annular illumination results when the most collimated portion of the light is removed. In the case of ASML equipment, no light is lost because the angle of the most collimated light is simply changed to fall within the distribution required. The center and left drawings illustrate different angular distributions of collimation, and both are void of any parallel rays of light. These are then said to be annular.
--[p327]-- The size of the “conventional” spot indicates how the light is directionally collimated: that is, how much the beam diverges from the axis of illumination as you move from one point along the beam to another point further on. A larger spot indicates the light diverges more. This condition is called “partial collimation”, and is indicated in ASML systems as  (sigma). More decollimation is the same as higher sigma. If collimation were perfect, the spot would be a point.

===== [p80-81] FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Frequency Response of a Weak Spring (4块) =====
--[p81]-- Air is introduced at the desired pressure, via the air valve.
The membrane is a flexible seal that allows the piston to move whilst maintaining the pressure in the body of the airmount. Thus an air cushion is created to damp vibrations.
Lorentz motors - The are able to move the piston plate in the vertical and horizontal plane. These are water cooled.
Geophones - are extremely sensitive motion detectors. They monitor in both horizontal and vertical directions.
--[p81]-- They monitor in both horizontal and vertical directions.
Together with the Lorentz motors they form an active system to compensate for vibrations in the silent world.
Horizontal and vertical proximity sensors - show the relative position of the silent world with respect to the external world.
There are mechanical hard stops for each airmount that physically limit horizontal and vertical movement.
--[p80]-- Hold up a piece of elastic with a weight attached to one end.
If you move your hand slowly, the weight will follow (low frequencies will pass through). However if you move your hand quickly the weight will stay still (higher frequencies are damped out). This demonstrates the purpose of a low stiffness suspension system (one with a low resonant frequency). High frequency vibrations are filtered out. The resonant frequency of the airmounts should be below 2Hz.
Air is introduced at the desired pressure, via the air valve.
--[p80]-- 1. Constant amplitude - no amplification
2. Resonance - maximum amplification
3. Above resonant frequency - attenuation or damping
The amplification versus vibration frequency relationship for a spring is sketched in the above slide. Three regions are visible.
1. Constant amplitude - no amplification
2. Resonance - maximum amplification
3. Above resonance - attenuation or damping
You can see this for yourself. Hold up a piece of elastic with a weight attached to one end.

===== [p116-116] Scanning Wafer Stage (SWS) > Stage Motors (X,Y movement) (3块) =====
--[p116]--  Accelerate stages to desired speed
– Maximum reticle stage scan speed of 1 Ms-1required
 Maintain constant speed during exposure
 Maintain desired scan direction
 Maintain desired stage rotation
 Accurately place stages
– Stage horizontal position can be measured to within 0.6 nm
 Not transfer vibrations to stages
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
The stages are moved in X and Y by the stage motors. The mass of the reticle stage is approximately 26 kg. That of the wafer stage is approximately 44 kg.
--[p116]-- That of the wafer stage is approximately 44 kg.
The reticle stage needs to be accelerated from rest within a few mm. This clearly requires a powerful motor. The motors need to move the stages over a considerable distance in the required direction and to position the stage at the desired location with extreme accuracy. The horizontal position of the stages is measured by interferometers. These can determine the stage position to approximately 0.6nm.
As discussed in the Airmounts presentation the stages need to be in the silent world.
--[p116]-- As discussed in the Airmounts presentation the stages need to be in the silent world.
Therefore the powerful motors that move the stages at high speed over a relatively large distance must do so without introducing any vibration.
Each stage motor has two components. These are a long stroke motor and a short stroke motor. How these work together to achieve the desired stage movement is discussed below.

--[p439]-- Dynamic effects are different, because as the wafer scans through the exposure area, each point on the wafer is exposed to different errors at different points on the lens. This tends to average out the effect.
--[p439]-- Dynamic Errors are stage movement induced errors.
The effect can only be seen during scanning exposures
Static effects are effectively the same as for a stepper, because the stages are not moving during exposure, so the errors in the exposure area of the lens are directly reproduced on the wafer.
However, while the stepper uses the whole lens area for exposures, on the scanner the exposure area is normally confined to the size of the exposure slit, so the areas of worst distortion are not used.

Therefore multiple windows running different programs can be displayed at any time.
As Solaris makes extensive use of swap files, it is important that the system is shut down correctly before the work station is switched off. Failure to do so may result in damage to the hard disk. Therefore OCU is equipped with an uninterruptable power supply (UPS). This provides sufficient power for the system to shut down correctly if main power is lost.
--[p388]-- v means verbose and provides information about what the command is doing.
f indicates the device on which the archive is to be created/read/extracted the default at ASML being the floppy.
When working with tar archives it is necessary to specify a path to the device where the archive will be/is located, eg
tar cvf to.backup /dev/rdiskette0
will create a tar archive of the file to.backup on the floppy disk the path to which is /dev/rdiskette0.
Note on some models of workstation, the eject command (/eject) has to be used to remove floppies from the drive.
Such models can be identified by the absence of an eject button on the disk drive.

--[p76]-- Due to the telecentricity of the lens the position of the image plane depends on the object plane.
So, once the object plane is determined by the previous tests the LS height and tilt can be determined. The LS height can be adjusted to the image plane by a rotation of the offset plane plates that are present in the optical path of the LS. The tilt is adjusted by adding a height to each LS spot measurement i.e. feedforward correction.
--[p74]-- On the steppers, the FEM is used as a tool to check for changes in focus at wafer level (best focus, LS height and tilt, etc).
With the scanner, this can also be true, but the FEM is now also used during system setup as a means of measuring the image plane both above, and below the lens.
Firstly, the scanner has a scanning reticle stage.
This means that the reticle position is no longer fixed, as it was on a stepper.
--[p122]-- To keep track of the drift of the machine with respect to the reference state we can use the image sensor.
The image sensor can be set up to correct for focus parameters only (focus correction), or for focus and intra field parameters (system correction).
In general the results for focus are very good. But the reproducibility of the IS (Image sensor) in the horizontal plane is not so good. This is mainly due to the influence of the global alignment noise on the measurements.
--[p76]-- Also in this test the wafer is read out with a microscope at the four corners of each exposed slit.
During measurement it must again be taken into account that, depending on the type of microscope, the wafer can be point mirrored in the ocular. The measured values are put in the PAS software that will calculate the level sensor height and tilt. The offset plane plates are set to correct for LS height. The LS tilt is corrected by feed forwards.
--[p112]-- Third order distortion is corrected by adjusting the reticle stage height, E1 and E2.
The reticle stage rotation and scaling can be deducted from the overlay, from 936 marks, between the second and first layer.
When needed the MCs are updated. More information can be found in the application note: “Scanning RS setup: Stage Rotation”
This test creates an image sensor reference state. The measured aerial image coincides with best calibrated imaging performance so far. The IS reference MCs is a set of X, Y & Z positions of IS marks.

--[p73]-- In i-line machines the image quality driver uses a model to correct for pressure changes.
The sensitivities of image errors to pressure and temperature variation are known and used to calculate the corrections the machine has to make, i.e. the feedforwards (lens element heights, reticle stage height, LS height and Red Blue offsets).
The FEM on a scanner is very different from the steppers. The purpose is still to find the best focus and energy within a matrix, but it is now used in a lot more ways.
--[p114]-- The IS reference MCs is a set of X, Y & Z positions of IS marks.
Metrology tests that change the IQ MCs automatically perform this image sensor scan during an update of the MCs when there is already a reference state present.
Only when the reference state defined for image sensor under MC/IQ/Criterion data is set to “No”, these tests will not automatically perform an image sensor scan.
The test is added especially for the fact that the image sensor scan during some metrology tests might fail when this MC is set to “Yes”.
--[p73]-- A different correction method is chosen for i-line machines (/400).
Since it is not possible to adjust the wavelength at all and since the i-line lenses are purged with Helium only (to reduce the chromatic aberrations which is not fully accomplished by the different quartz type lenses). In i-line machines the image quality driver uses a model to correct for pressure changes.
--[p112]-- The first layer aligned positions, from 468 marks, are compared to the expected positions, so called first to nominal modeling.
This gives us the intrafield parameters, magnification, rotation, translation and third order distortion.
Rotation, translation and magnification are corrected by offsets to the actual alignment beam position with respect to the DUV aerial image (Red Blue offsets).
Third order distortion is corrected by adjusting the reticle stage height, E1 and E2.

The Setup\_Imagin\_SC reticle is kept on a fixed X, Y and Z-position (the object plane), while the wafer is statically exposed at different focus steps (image plane). ===== [p338-338] Introduction to semiconductor industry FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Electronic components (8块) =====
--[p338]-- It decreases with size because the individual switching time of each amplifier decreases and also because the total travelling distance becomes shorter. Switching speed is important because it restricts the amount of calculations or actions per second that can be done. (Refer to the notes on slide ‘Building a chip’ for background on the following:) Switching speed is these days foremost limited by the ‘gate’ width.
--[p338]-- The purpose of this slide is to provide basic understanding of what integrated circuits are and do, and why industry likes to make them as small as possible.
Electronics evolved from vacuum tubes to transistors to modern integrated circuits or IC’s. The basic issue in all of these is that a small input signal controls a (larger) output signal. We speak of amplifiers.
--[p338]-- For example: an ‘AND gate’ yields a high output only if BOTH input signals are high, hence its name.
Combining many logical components like this ‘AND gate’ in specific ways can make the resulting device function like, for instance, a calculator. As more and more complex devices like these were constructed, the ‘computer’ evolved.
The most basic difference between the old (vacuum tube) computers and modern microcomputers (using integrated circuits) is the nature of the amplifiers that constitute the logical components.
--[p338]-- Going from vacuum tubes to transistors to modern integrated circuits, component size, component switching time and component power consumption decrease drastically. Switching time (inversely related to the more commonly used term ‘switching speed’) can be thought of as the time it takes for a signal to be transmitted through all components it encounters.
--[p338]-- The gate area sets up the electric field that allows or cuts off the source-drain current. The smaller this area, the less charge is needed to set up the electric field. This means that the same current to/from the gate will set up the necessary field in less time, making the component function faster.
We conclude that we would like to make a device (independent of functionality and design) as small as we can, to make it as fast as possible.
--[p338]-- Modern computers contain integrated circuits with as many as possible features build into the systems main processor(s). The Pentium processor is a well known example with well over 4 million transistors on it.
--[p338]-- Electronics evolved from vacuum tubes to transistors to integrated circuits or IC’s.
 The electronic units became:
– smaller,
(IC technology)
– faster,
(smaller, components closer together)
– less power consuming. (unit/component redesign)
Silicon technology v.s. Gallium-Arsenide technology: fast & expensive v.s. even faster & far more expensive.
So: Modern industry makes (silicon) IC’s.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
--[p338]-- We speak of amplifiers.
However, their usage does not end with simply ‘amplifying’ signals: combining several amplifiers, so called ‘logical devices’ can be constructed. This is done by making the output of two amplifiers control the input of a third. Depending on the way the interconnection is made, this combined component gives a high or low output signal depending on whether the input signals are high and/or low. For example: an ‘AND gate’ yields a high output only if BOTH input signals are high, hence its name.

===== [p141-141] Stage Movement Design > Cause of cogging: (1块) =====
--[p141]-- Residual magnetism in coils. The field strength of the coils is not exactly what you ‘think’ it is due to the presence of residual fields.
The highly localized positions of the magnetic poles. The attracting/repelling force varies with the distance between the magnetic poles.

===== [p113-115] Scanning Wafer Stage (SWS) > Contents (5块) =====
--[p115]-- The reticle is moved through a slit of illumination above the lens.
Thus a changing image is projected to wafer level. Here the wafer is moved through the image slit. Note that the image at wafer level is four times smaller in the X direction than the features on the reticle: magnification in the Y direction is determined by scan speed. The image is also inverted. The relative scan velocities and scan directions of the reticle and the wafer need to be precisely controlled in order that the desired image is captured on the wafer.
--[p115]-- Consider the effect on overlay if the start position of a scan varied between exposures.
The absolute stage velocity determines the time each point on the reticle and wafer spend in the slit. Therefore it is the scan speed that determines exposure dose for a given illumination intensity.
How the essential control over the stages is achieved is discussed below.

===== [p211-211] Alignment > Overview of the alignment system: (4块) =====
--[p211]-- Please note that the actual spot size is larger than the size of an individual mark, it is illuminating the mark completely.
The diffracted first order image (in X and Y) of one alignment mark from the wafer passes through the projection lens, several optical correction elements, through the reticle, and enters the alignment block. All higher orders are filtered out by a spatial filter, and the zero order is reflected back to the laser.
--[p211]-- The light beam splits in the alignment block.
One beam continues to the modulator and detector as described above.
The other beam (small percentage of the total intensity) is directed at a video camera and can be seen on the monitor of the operator console.
The position of the two stages is measured by the stage interferometers.
During alignment all data gathered on the actual position of marks is based on position readings from the interferometers.
--[p211]-- The system uses a Helium-Neon laser (633 nm) coherent light source. The alignment laser beam passes through the illumination case where it is split into two parallel optical beams. From the illumination case, the beams are directed through a window in the side of the projection lens.
A reflective prism and a mirror, in the projection lens, direct the two alignment beams toward the wafer.
--[p211]-- At the reticle, the reflected image of the wafer alignment mark is projected on the corresponding alignment mark on the reticle. In the alignment block, the wafer/reticle image passes through the alignment block optics and then finally focuses onto a detector. The wafer is perfectly aligned if the signals from the two images (wafer mark and reticle mark) are evenly detected by the detector. The light beam splits in the alignment block.

===== [p355-355] CONTAMINATION & TEMPERATURE CONTROL (C&T) > Water System (4) (2块) =====
--[p355]-- • A flowmeter provides and indication of the cooling water flow. It is imperative to monitor the flowmeter so that flow stays within range to prevent cooling problems of the ellipsoidal mirror.
--[p355]--  Water-cooling System Bottom Module (/400 only)
• Cooling water is also used for cooling the mirror of the lamp and the shutter in the bottom module of an I-line system with AERIAL illumination (/400).
• Most of the heat generated by the mercury lamp is removed by cooling water (about 60%, the remaining 40% is removed by air flows). As the flow needed is considerable (360 I/h), it is implemented as a separate connection to the fab cooling water.

--[p418]-- Caused by a rotated wafer.
Measured by the alignment system.
Correction by rotation of the E-chuck (all systems)
Compare these to the drawings of the intrafield errors
Symmetric scaling error: fields are shifted with regard to the center of the wafer and the shift of the field increases with the distance of the field to the center.
Occurs when the stage doesn’t move accurately (e.g.. all steps are to large).
Caused by interferometer.
Determined during calibration of the interferometer.
--[p417]-- Interfield error: between the fields caused by wafer, wafer stage, HP system. (only in X/Yplane) For those errors, we can separate them into two groups: symmetric errors and asymmetric errors.
Translation error: all images displaced in X and/or Y.
Caused by a translated wafer.
Measured by alignment.
Correction: adjusting the wafer stage position.
Same result as intrafield translation error.
Rotation error: all fields are rotated around the center of the wafer (wafer is rotated on the E-table).
--[p415]-- This is a real lens property.
Different focal distance between center and edge of image field, focal plane is three dimensional plane.
Caused by the lens.
Determined with a FEM or FOCAL test.
Taken into account when determining the Usable Depth Of Focus (UDOF).

Correctable with lens element 1, lens element 2 and reticle table height.
Caused by the lens.
Correction: NCE. (it’s a lens property)
Determined with FOCAL or FEM test.
Translation , scaling and rotation error
--[p420]-- Translation error: some images displaced in X or Y.
Caused by contamination of mirror or wrong (re)calibration of the mirror. Correction: cleaning, re-calibration, …
Cause: could be any (instant) influences for wafer stage movements and/or HP system (such as: airshower works not well, instant changes of environment, …) or any combination of other errors.
Correction: find the specific cause and take effective measures.
Asymmetric scaling error: like symmetric scaling but absolute movements of the stage differ in X and Y.
--[p423]-- Caused by non-orthogonality of the mirror block.
Measured by Non-orthogonality/Scaling test.
Compensated for by adjusting the CD (Coordinate system control) machine constants for non-orthogonality.
--[p422]-- Occurs when the stage doesn’t move accurately (e.g. all steps are too large in one direction).
Determined by Non-orthogonality/Scaling test.
Compensated for by the CD (Coordinate system control) machine constants (like symmetric scaling), offset on steps.
Asymmetric rotation (or Non-orthogonality) error: all fields have the rotated position (wrong position) in one direction (you can see “shifts” parallel to the mirror) but have the right position in another direction.
--[p414]-- Not depending on refractive index changes.
Determined during calibration with a FEM test or FOCAL.
Periodically verified by the image sensor.
Recalibration with FEM, FOCAL or with image sensor.
Correction: the wafer is tilted (after the tilt is determined with a FEM) (all systems), LS tilt Machine Constant is updated.
Field curvature (FC).
Field curvature = z between centre-best-focus and the average focus of the four corners
Focal Plane Deviation (FPD) =  z between highest and lowest focus position. This is a real lens property.
--[p419]-- Determined during calibration of the interferometer.
Compensated for by the CD (Coordinate system control) machine constants. Absolute corrections (grid corrections) are used to correct for errors on a “single” machine and matching corrections (grid matching) are used to match different machines.
Note: If the wafer is expanded (due to the process), one can see that the expansion error will like the scaling error + magnification error.
Translation error: some images displaced in X or Y.

===== [p489-491] Dose Control FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Intensity [ mW/cm 2 ] with Uniformity Test (6块) =====
--[p490]-- The purpose of the Scanning Dose Accuracy & Repeatability Test is to compare the requested dose with the dose received by the Spot Sensor during a scan. Here we use dynamic mode, but only in one direction in Y (forward). The notes of the next slide will explain why we test only in one direction.
The Wafer Stage including the Spot Sensor and the REMA blades are moved during the scan in order to simulate a regular Die exposure.
--[p491]-- For each dose, the scan is repeated 100 times in one direction (forwards).
The spot sensor position on the mirror block is critical for the scanning waferstage. To position the spot sensor underneath the lens the stage is near a mechanical endstop. Therefore we only scan in one direction, to avoid any problems for this test.
The following data will be collected :
– - Sum of Energy Sensor samples
– - Sum of Spot Sensor samples
– - Number of samples
--[p491]-- The higher the dose, the slower the speed.
At low dose (< 17 mJ/cm2 with 250 mm/s and < 34 mJ/cm2 with 125 mm/s)) the pulse energy is attenuated with a variable attenuator in the optical path (bottom module), in order to get the proper accuracy. The stage speed is limited to a maximum speed of 250 mm/s (phase2) and 125 mm/s (phase 1).
The measurement is done dynamically when the Wafer Stage moves. For each dose, the scan is repeated 100 times in one direction (forwards).
--[p489]-- X = 26 mm
Static Measurements matrix : 11 ( X ) x 3 ( Y ) only at the top of the intensity profile
• Measured with Spot Sensor
• Conventional and Annular settings
spec = 600 mW/cm2 (conv. and annu.)
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
To measure the Intensity, we use the same Slit Uniformity Test, only this time we only measure 3 points in the each of the 11 lines.
Again, we measure 100 pulses at each spot, to minimize the effect of pulse energy variation
--[p489]-- For each line, the average of the 3 points is taken, and these values used to calculate the mean intensity.
Notice that the intensity profile on the right is trapezoidal, and we are only measuring on the top of the trapezoid.
The reason for this shape is that it is necessary for the dose control to avoid quantisation effects, which will be discussed later
To measure the intensity during a scanning exposure, we use the Dose Repeatability and Accuracy Check.
--[p490]-- The difference with a regular scan is that the user defines an imaginary Die including the Spot Sensor which is moved through the slit while firing laser pulses. The Spot Sensor can be located on any position of the imaginary Die and is measuring the total integrated energy across the slit.
The test is executed at stage speeds equivalent to a nominal dose of 5, 10 , 20, 30, 40, 50, 100 and 150 mJ/cm2. The higher the dose, the slower the speed.

===== [p133-134] Stage Movement Design > – Zeroing the coordinate system. (7块) =====
--[p134]-- Reticle is aligned.
Fine pre-alignment and all subsequent exchanges achieve reticle placement well within the capture range of the alignment system.
--[p134]-- Fine pre-align reticle (over the RPAS Quadcells)
R-pins move up (z) and takes over reticle.
Gripper retracts g-pins and moves a few mm to the front of the machine.
R-pins with reticle move down (z), the reticle is clamped by the vacuum pads on the reticle chuck. R-pins move further down and out of the way. Gripper moves to the left and out of the way.
Reticle is aligned.
--[p134]-- The reader is referred to the alignment and coordinate systems presentations.
The loading sequence of a reticle has already been discussed in the ARMS module. This slide only provides a quick overview of where the Reticle Stage parts that are needed for this are located.
In short the sequence is:
Gripper takes reticle out of library
Coarse pre-align reticle
Move stage into load position (gap in center is moved over Reticle-pins) This is a known distance (Machine Constant) from the zero position which is determined during initialization (see next slide).
--[p133]-- This has a measurement resolution of 10 µm.
Thus the reticle chuck fiducials H1 and H2 are brought close to the fine pre-alignment quad cells on the top of the lens. The short stroke motors are then released to pre-align the reticle chuck using the marks on the fiducials. Here the interferometers are set to nominal (zero) position. Finally the stone is moved to it’s calibrated nominal height.
--[p133]-- Finally the stone is moved to it’s calibrated nominal height.
The accuracy with which the initial X,Y position can be reproduced as determined by the reticle pre-alignment system is approximately three microns. This is sufficient to ensure that the reticle stage fiducials can be brought within the capture range of the alignment system. The absolute X Y position of the reticle stage is defined in terms of the wafer stage coordinate system during stage to stage alignment. The reader is referred to the alignment and coordinate systems presentations.
--[p133]-- Short stroke motor presses reticle chuck against buffers.
The long stroke motor is initialized.
 The long stroke motors place pre-alignment marks on H1 and H2 close to the quad cells.
 The short stroke motor pre-aligns the stage over the quad-cells. The interferometers are set to their calibrated values at this position.
 The stone is moved to its calibrated nominal height.

Initialization determines the coordinate system that the reticle stage moves in prior to alignment, that is the reticle stage coordinate system.
Initialization starts with the reticle chuck being clamped to the long stroke motor arm. This ensures that the stage doesn’t waggle about during the long stroke motor initialization. As the long stroke motor is initialized it’s position is determined by the linear encoder. This has a measurement resolution of 10 µm.

===== [p339-339] Introduction to semiconductor industry FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > How are IC’s made? (6块) =====
--[p339]-- There have been size standards of 1and 2 cm, 1, 11½, 2½, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8 and 12 inch wafers. Currently, 8 inch wafers is the standard in most wafer fabs. The next standard is 12 inch. ASML’s AT series machines are designed to process 12 inch wafers.
Chips are constructed on top of (and in) the wafer surface area. Materials are being deposited on top of the silicon and ‘dopants’ are shot (or diffused) into the surface area to create semiconducting components in the silicon crystal.
--[p339]--  IC’s (or chips) are mass produced on wafers.
Wafers are thin slices of silicon, polished flat. Chips are constructed on top of (and in) the wafer surface area.
The point of this slide is to explain the relation between the IC and its ‘nursery’, the wafer.
Wafers are thin slices of silicon, polished to a desired degree of flatness. The silicon is grown as a single crystal cylinder: a cylinder of silicon is rotating in a silicon bath, growing as it is being pulled out slowly.
--[p339]-- The metal pins protruding from the sides of the package are connected to the chip within.
The pins will fit into a socket on a printed circuit board. The circuit board connects the chip to its power source, its input devices and its output devices. The size of the package is usually dictated by the number of pins on it. Contrary to what one might expect, these days the package is often far more expensive than the actual chip inside. Increased wafer area lowers the cost per chip drastically, because an increasingly large number of chips may be processed at once.
--[p339]-- A die is the unit on the wafer that will become a single integrated circuit.
The rightmost picture displays packaged integrated circuits prior to being cut from the lead frame (a support mechanism for packaging automation). The final physical step is then lead forming, which bends the package pins into the proper configuration for PC board installation. The chip is safely contained within the package. The actual ‘chip’ can be quite a bit smaller than its package. The metal pins protruding from the sides of the package are connected to the chip within.
--[p339]-- On top of these electronically active areas isolating oxide layers and conducting metal layers are deposited to interconnect selected areas only and create contact points on the topside of the chip.
The left two pictures depict wafers that are fully processed. The dies are finished and can be cut from the wafer (which is when we start calling them chips). A die is the unit on the wafer that will become a single integrated circuit.
--[p339]-- Packaging, however, requires handling each chip on an individual basis.

===== [p210-210] Alignment > Alignment concept: (2块) =====
--[p210]-- Therefore before exposing any new layer, the wafer is aligned to the reticle.
This means that the wafer stage position is determined at which the centre of the wafer is exactly below the centre of the reticle. Additionally, the wafer stage rotates the wafer such that the X-axes of reticle and wafer are parallel. Using this start position, any section of the wafer can be positioned underneath the reticle for exposure.
--[p210]-- The alignment system aligns a wafer to the reticle using two or more alignment marks. Before exposing the next layer on a wafer, the wafer must be positioned, with respect to the reticle image, in exactly the same way as it was positioned during exposure of the previous layer. Only in this way can accurate overlay be guaranteed. Therefore before exposing any new layer, the wafer is aligned to the reticle.

There are several parameters that influence the calculation of the optimized route. On the slide, some of these are displayed.
--[p197]-- On the slide, some of these are displayed.
These parameters are used to determine the optimized route. In the algorithm for optimized route the level sensor plays a big role. For the route calculation algorithm to be robust enough, we want to calculate an optimized route that is not changing if we change machine type, exposure dose etc. The exposure dose and therefore the actual scan speed are not in this algorithm. Also other parameters are hardcoded in Level Control (LC = level sensor driver).
--[p197]-- Also other parameters are hardcoded in Level Control (LC = level sensor driver).
Therefore this means that every optimized route is a sub-optimal one.

===== [p308-308] TRANSMISSION IMAGE SENSOR (TIS) > Capture Range (4块) =====
--[p308]-- The RIS capture range is limited because the detected reflected signals are periodic, therefore even though there could be a signal from a detector it could be in an erroneous position (e.g. focus jumps). However in the case of TIS, the measured signal is not periodic. If the mark is not within the predefined capture range, the scan will fail to detect the mark position.
The default scan for TIS is the fine scan (FineHorVerScan) which has a capture range of 0.25-m.
--[p308 [表]]-- RIS (DUV) | TIS
x, y | ± 4μm | ±4μm
Z | ± 8μm | ± 4μm
Small capture range in TIS is resolved by an error recovery algorithm.
– If the scan fails, the scan range is changed automatically.
TIS | RIS (DUV)
FineHorVerScan | MediumHorVerScan | CoarseHorVerScan
x, y | ± 0.25μm | ±0.5μm | ± 4μm | --
Z | ±1μm | ±1.6μm | ± 4μm | ± 4μm
--[p308]-- The default scan for TIS is the fine scan (FineHorVerScan) which has a capture range of 0.25-m.
Normally there is no need for a larger capture range, since the global alignment has sufficient accuracy and the best focus is already known within 0.2-m during the normal system set-up.
However, the capture range sometimes needs to be larger than the range of the fine scan if the focus is not within the capture range.
--[p308]-- So, two new scan types have been introduced, a medium scan (MediumHorVerScan) and a coarse scan (CoarseHorVerScan), which have much larger ranges permitting sufficient capture range.
In normal machine operation, the fine scan is employed. However should the scan fail (i.e. out of range), an auto recovery algorithm will take over, as shown on the slide. (For release 8.4 and higher.)
So, the small capture range issue of the TIS default scan is resolved by this error recovery algorithm.
If this happens it will show up in the error logging; each scan that is made is logged.
For older software releases the schedule shown below is used.

– Wafer is moved (stepped) to the next exposure position.

===== [p340-340] Introduction to semiconductor industry FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Building a chip (7块) =====
--[p340]-- Details of processing will be provided in the metrology week of the SAP1 course. The purpose of this slide is merely to indicate that we need some kind of ‘blocking’ method to be able to make electronic components on a wafer. Currently, lithography is used to make this vital step.
A bit of background on IC components design:
The picture displays a Metal Oxide Semiconductor Field Effect Transistor or MOSFET.
--[p340]-- The picture displays a Metal Oxide Semiconductor Field Effect Transistor or MOSFET.
The first three letters in the acronym indicate the basic materials needed to make the device. The metal as the conductor, the oxide to isolate and the semiconductor for the electronic properties of the device. The last three letters indicate the working principle of the transistor. Externally, a ‘high’ voltage is applied between source and drain. The area between source and drain can be depleted of electrons if a large negative voltage is applied to the gate connector above it.
--[p340]-- In general, designs are optimized to minimize switching speed and power consumption.
Power consumption needs to be minimized because the transistor’s behavior is very much temperature dependent and power consumption heats up the IC during operation.
If we look at how well our MOSFET in the above drawing does with respect to switching speed, it basically depends on gate capacitance which increases with gate area. If we look at power consumption, it is minimal in the depleted state: no current is flowing.
--[p340]-- Basically, the CMOS is build up of two devices ‘like’ the MOSFET.
One is ‘complementary’ to the other. Both are interconnected to form a single device. This interconnection is made in such a way that the device has binary output states (e.g. high or low output voltage), and no current flow is necessary to maintain the output state. Hence power consumption is low in both output states! Only the remaining switching current and ‘current leakage’ are left to heat up the device. These are minimized by fine tuning the design.
--[p340]-- The electric field forces them away, interrupting current flow. Hence the ‘Field Effect’. With no current flowing between them, the source-drain voltage remains high. So, the gate input voltage controls the source-drain output voltage: a negative/positive gate yields a high/low source-drain potential.
There are also other designs to build transistors in IC’s. Each design has advantages and disadvantages. In general, designs are optimized to minimize switching speed and power consumption.
--[p340]--  Deposit layers of materials in/on the wafer.
 To build small structures we need to be able to block off selected parts of the wafer.
– Separate regions that should have different electrical characteristics.
– Create conducting lines (to act as wires) between different regions (and isolate these lines from each other).
The size of the component (and with it, component speed,) depends on the resolution at which you can perform this blocking off.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
--[p340]-- If we look at power consumption, it is minimal in the depleted state: no current is flowing.
In conductive state, however, a current is constantly flowing from source to drain to lower the source-drain voltage. Hence in a MOSFET, a constant power consumption is present in the low output state, heating up the IC.
Another much used design is CMOS or Complementary Metal Oxide Semiconductor. Here, the working principle of how the input signal controls the output signal is slightly different. Basically, the CMOS is build up of two devices ‘like’ the MOSFET.

===== [p348-348] CONTAMINATION & TEMPERATURE CONTROL (C&T) > C&T Systems (5块) =====
--[p348]-- Air exhaust prevents damage to electrical components due to high temperatures.
• Lens purging is used to maintain and protect the lens. Purging is a preventative action that prevents the components from degrading, and removes particles. For lens purging in i-line, a small amount of ozone is mixed into helium gas that flows through the lens to remove contaminants.
• Illumination purging is used to protect the optical components of the illumination system and to control their temperature. It is also used to lower the ozone concentration in the components of DUV systems.
--[p348]-- It is also used to lower the ozone concentration in the components of DUV systems.
The optical components are purged using clean air (i-line) or nitrogen (DUV) gas.
--[p348]-- Also the other function is to increase interferometer accuracy.
• Air extraction is used to remove particles created in the moving components of the exposure unit (motor yokes, wafer transport system, level sensor) by extracting (suction) the air to remove particles.
• Air exhaust is used to remove excess heat generated by the electronic components of the machine (like CPU board), and the mercury lamp using air fans, valves and the fab exhaust. Air exhaust prevents damage to electrical components due to high temperatures.
--[p348]--  Temperature Controlled Water
 Clean Air
 Extraction
 Air Exhaust
 Lens purging
 Illumination purging
Contamination & Temperature Control 48142 v00, 11/04/00 4
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
• Temperature controlled water is used for cooling parts of the machine that generate heat during their operation, such as the coils of the motors on the system or the electromagnets of the airmounts.
--[p348]-- Water is also used to prevent components from getting heated (lens, optical components near the mercury lamp), and to control the air temperature by means of heat exchangers.
• Clean air is supplied to airshowers that create a constant laminar (down) flow of air to certain components. Prevents contamination of wafers and reticles and helps stabilize the air temperature. Also the other function is to increase interferometer accuracy.

--[p329]-- For the other parts of the reticle the exposure light is blocked.

--[p329]-- – The quartz rod, which makes sure the light source that illuminates the reticle is of high uniformity.

===== [p226-226] Alignment > Scan scaling: (3块) =====
--[p226]-- Measurement of the actual distance with respect to the expected distance between H1 and G1 gives information about the scan scaling.
Two alignment scans are needed: F2 - H1 is scanned to measure the position of H1 with respect to the wafer stage grid.
Next, the reticle stage travels a defined distance in its own grid, under reticle stage yinterferometer control. The distance to travel (y-distance) between H1 and G1 is a known machine constant. Wafer stage mark F2 stays in position.
--[p226]-- Wafer stage mark F2 stays in position.
F2 - G1 is scanned to measure the position of G1 after the reticle stage travel.
Any difference between the traveled defined distance (measured with the yinterferometer) and the found y-position of mark G1 is a result of scaling, which is literally a scaling effect caused by a difference in interferometer readings between wafer and reticle stages.
This will be corrected for by modifying the start and end y-positions of the wafer stage.
--[p226]-- This will be corrected for by modifying the start and end y-positions of the wafer stage.
The y-coordinates (begin and end) of the wafer stage are modified to make sure that the wafer travels exactly the distance needed to expose the image on the correct position at the wafer.

===== [p195-203] LEVEL SENSOR (LS) FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Scanner Routing (13块) =====
--[p203]-- Since now the optimized route is calculated at the beginning of the batch and not during the job definition, the value it uses should be always the one that overrules the others according to the rule mentioned above.
--[p201]-- Scanner routing only executed if scanner software is installed
If stepper job created with scanner software, the optimized route can be determined.
Consequences: create SAWS for machine type scanner if the optimized route is needed.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL

--[p195]--  Scanner Routing Algorithm:
– In Job Definition during Image Distribution
– Executed (optionally) per image
– At finish of Job Creation, non-optimized routes can still be optimized
– Consequences: scanner route optimization is per image => multiple image jobs suffer from sub-optimal scanner routing
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
An optimized route can be calculated for each image. So if in 1 job 4 different images exist it is possible to calculate 4 optimized routes, 1 for each image.
--[p199]--  Scanner Routing Execution
– Optimized route in Batch Control fails if machine constants of WS and LS are not equal to hard-coded values in Level Control (LC). If optimized route not executed, Exposure Control determines the scanner meander route which is executed.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
If the values in level control are different from the machine constants, the calculation of the optimized route will fail.
--[p201]-- If the standard route is used, the focus edge clearance from Factory Constants / Batch Control Data / Focus Edge Clearance is used.
If the optimized route calculation is required, create the job with scanner software. The optimized route will NOT be executed on a stepper. So if different layers of a job are exposed on steppers and scanners, the optimized route can be exposed on scanners and the standard route will be used on steppers.
--[p199]-- If the focus edge clearance in Factory Constants / Batch Control Data is different from the Factory Constants / Job Definition Data, it is possible that the calculated optimized route will not be executed.
--[p195]-- In image distribution there are 2 buttons for this purpose:
route
optimize
de-optimize
So you can optimize or de-optimize the route per image. Sometimes the calculation of the optimized route can not be performed. For instance when some machine constants are set to wrong values or when not enough images are distributed during image distribution in job definition (for example just 2).
--[p201]-- If a job is created with stepper software, it is impossible to expose layers of this job on a scanner with an optimized route.
Exposing a wafer with non optimized route:
If you use the non optimized route (worst case scenario for the /400 slit), the critical percentage value is not taken into account.
Usage of the critical percentage:
The critical percentage value can be modified in
-Factory Constants/ Job Definition Data
-Job Definition
-Batch Control
--[p196]-- In this case, reference information is needed from an adjacent field.
If such a field is not present, a measurement scan is performed on an imaginary field which is adjacent to the field to be exposed. If this is the case, the calculation of the optimized route will fail and the normal route will be executed.
--[p203]-- -Job Definition
In Job Definition when a New Job is created, it is possible to define a critical percentage for each layer different from the default value defined in Factory Constants. The critical percentage value in the job will overrule the critical percentage value in the factory constants. In batch control the software takes the critical percentage from the job and then is possible again to overrule it.
--[p200]-- The level sensor functionality displayed here (inner, intra and inter) is applicable for TILT only (so not for height).
Also during execution of the batch the progress and therefore the route can be seen.
When the batch is finished, there is a file that contains the route in the LC directory. For instance LC/LCDR\_image\_1. This is an ASCII file which is rather complicated to read.
--[p195]-- When not enough images are distributed, it is possible that measurement scan are required which will lead to the optimized route calculation to fail. In these cases the normal route is still available.
If just a few fields are exposed and these fields are close to the wafer edge, this means these fields could be inter for height and/or tilt. In this case, reference information is needed from an adjacent field.
--[p200]--  Scanner Routing Visualization
– in Job Definition, including die type and scan direction.
– During the execution of the batch
– After batch execution: LC/LCRD\_image\_1 Note: this is an ASCII file in a very unfriendly format.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
The optimized route can be seen in Job Definition / Layer Layout / Reticle Data. By pushing a button a graphics screen will come up displaying the route, including scan direction and inner/intra/inter level sensor functionality.

===== [p118-118] Stage Movement Design > The Master and Slave Relationship (3块) =====
--[p118]-- – The difference sensor signal controls movement of the long stroke motor
The X,Y position of both scanning stages is monitored and controlled by interferometers. When a new interferometer step point is established, that is a new horizontal stage position requested, the master (short stroke motor) moves towards that position. This movement crates a difference sensor signal. The signal is proportional to the displacement of the displacement of the short stroke motor moving body relative to the stationary body.
--[p118]-- The output from the difference sensor controls the long stroke motor. The force exerted by the long stroke motor is proportional to the difference sensor output.
--[p118]--  The stage position is under interferometer control
 The short stroke motor is the master
 The long stroke motor is the slave
 When the stage needs to move to a new position
– The master starts to move to the new position
– The difference sensor detects a change in the relative positions of the short stroke motor bodies
– The difference sensor signal controls movement of the long stroke motor
ASML CONFIDENTIAL

===== [p48-48] FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Alignment Subsystem (5块) =====
--[p48]-- Through-The-Lens alignment is commonly referred to by its acronym: TTL. It makes use of a laser beam that travels from wafer level, through the lens, to reticle level. So mark positions are measured at wafer level, relative to the mark at reticle level.
The solid lines in the picture represent a laser beam. The small dark rectangles at the bottom of the picture represent wafer (stage) alignment marks.
--[p48]--  Through The Lens (shown in illustration)
– Directly positions wafer (stage) with respect to reticle (stage).
 Off-Axis
– Positions wafer mark with respect to Off-Axis module (not shown).
 Purpose:
– Correctly position wafer with respect to reticle.
– Correctly align scan axis of wafer stage and reticle stage.
ASML CONFIDENTIAL
The alignment subsystem is a measurement unit that is spread out through the machine.
--[p48]-- The small dark rectangles at the bottom of the picture represent wafer (stage) alignment marks.
The small dark rectangles in the laser beam path at the top of the picture represent reticle marks. Reticle stage marks are not indicated in the picture.
Off-Axis (OA):
The Off-Axis module measures mark position on wafer level, relative to a standard mark on the wafer stage. It was designed to make aligned position less dependent on wafer processing.
--[p48]-- The alignment subsystem is a measurement unit that is spread out through the machine.
The Through-The-Lens alignment system has laser beam projection parts inside and next to the projections lens. The detection unit is located above the reticle.
The Off-Axis module is mounted as a single unit underneath the sensor plate.
The purpose of the alignment system is to correctly position the wafer with respect to the reticle, and to ensure that the scan axis of both wafer stage and reticle stage are parallel.
--[p48]-- Since chips are made with typically 20+ lithographic steps (layers), the proper positioning (the overlay) of a wafer to match the previous layers is of vital importance to get a working chip. Note that the smaller the features on the chips get, the less margin there is for overlay errors. Overlay performance is one of the most important specifications for a lithographic tool. ASML uses special marks on wafers and reticles to position both with respect to each other.
Through-The-Lens (TTL):

===== [p57-59] FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Emergency Off buttons (4块) =====
--[p57]-- The laser source is also equipped with an emergency button.
The button is released by turning it and allowing it to jump back out.
Note that supplies that are not integral to the step & scan machine (Air for cooling, vacuum) are not interrupted. Also, the uninterruptable power source (UPS) of the operator console is uninfluenced. (The UPS will provide power for the console to properly shut down the software in case of a persisting power failure.)
Interlocks are placed on the machine frame and are pushed in by the covers attached on the frame.
--[p57]-- Interlocks are placed on the machine frame and are pushed in by the covers attached on the frame.
If any cover is removed or a door opened, the interlock is released, which closes the safety shutter of the light source.
A selection of laser sources that are used in the step & scan system.
Ozone (O3) is a toxic gas. Its creation from oxygen (O2) is stimulated by UV radiation.
Mercury (Hg) is a toxic metal. It is found in the /400 lamp. If the lamp is broken, leave the area and call for a special clean-up team.
The gasses helium (He), nitrogen (N2), Krypton (Kr) and neon (Ne) are not toxic.
--[p57]-- Each separate unit of the machine has at least one EMO (EMergency Off) button.
– When pressed, all power to the machine is instantly cut-off.
Interlocks on the covers will make the laser safety shutter close when a cover is removed.
The emergency power off button, when pressed, immediately cuts off all power to the machine. The main unit and all support units are instantly disconnected from their power source.
The laser source is also equipped with an emergency button.
--[p59]-- The gasses helium (He), nitrogen (N2), Krypton (Kr) and neon (Ne) are not toxic.
If any of them is released in large quantities, however, danger of suffocation arises from a reduced oxygen concentration.
Fluorine (F2) is a very dangerous gas, it reacts with almost everything. Even small concentrations of fluorine in the air can be lethal.
During service, cleaning fluids like ‘fischwasser’ and acetone may be used. Both are flammable. Acetone will absorb through the skin to cause liver damage.

===== [p62-62] Machine Access FOR TRAINING PURPOSES ONLY > Additional information: (2块) =====
--[p62]-- The motors that are used inside the Step & Scan system are very powerful. They can easily cause severe injuries when touched during operation.
Laser sources are dangerous for the eyes. Reflected or scattered laser light is still powerful enough to cause permanent injury.
Having covers removed from, or improperly attached to, the Step & Scan system will allow cleanroom conditions (pressure, temperature, possible contamination) to penetrate the machine to some extent.
--[p62]-- Only with all the covers in place is it guaranteed that the exposure conditions inside the system are as they should be. Temperature is a particularly critical issue, because the system contains some very high mass components which require significant time to re-equilibrate if they are allowed to change temperature.
The pressure inside of the scanner is kept at an higher value than the atmospheric pressure in the cleanroom.

===== [p122-122] Stage Movement Design > Reticle Chuck Layout (Top View) (3块) =====
--[p122]-- These are used during reticle stage initialization.
The Standard Reticle is a metrology reticle containing image sensor marks (see the image sensor presentations). It is fixed to the reticle chuck, as are the others.
Device reticles are clamped onto the reticle chuck surface with vacuum pads. Note that the alignment marks labeled ‘K’ are on the device reticle, not on the reticle chuck.
--[p122]-- The above picture is a schematic plan view of a reticle chuck. This is made from Zerodur, a material which displays minimal thermal expansion.
Five mark carriers are shown, along with a device reticle. These are all quartz plates. H1, H2, G1 and G2 are called fiducial plates. Alignment marks can be found on these fiducials. These along with U1 and U2 are used during alignment (see the alignment and image sensor presentations) H1 and H2 also carry reticle pre-alignment marks.
--[p122]-- Note that the alignment marks labeled ‘K’ are on the device reticle, not on the reticle chuck.
Note U1, H1, K1 and G1 are laid out along a straight line and can all be positioned in the alignment branch M1. U2, H2, K2 and G2 can all be placed in the path of alignment beam M2.

===== [p465-465] Effects of Lens Magnification > Moving Standard Deviation (MSD) (2块) =====
--[p465]-- The Moving Standard Deviation corresponds to fast (high frequency) stage errors; i.e. stage (relative) vibrations (noise) and therefore causes a fading of the image (compare with the effect of shaking a photo camera while releasing the shutter: the result will be a blurred (faded) photo).
The faded image has a reduced contrast and leads to CD variations.
--[p465]-- MSD causes fading and therefore affects the contrast. This will result in CD variation.
The Moving Standard Deviation (MSD) during the same time period determines the loss of contrast, or fading, which affects achievable resolution and CD control.

===== [p100-100] ADVANCED RETICLE MANAGEMENT SYSTEM (ARMS) > IRIS Parameters (5块) =====
--[p100]--  Particle bins
 Operator intervention
 Result lifetime
 Inspection surfaces
 Reject limit
 Automatic/manual scan
•For classification of the impact of particles, there are three “particle bins” specified in the PAS software: small, medium and large. In Factory Constants / IRIS Batch Control Data / Upper (Lower) Surface Particle Bin the minimum and maximum particle size for the three bins has to be specified for both the glass and pellicle surfaces.
--[p100]-- The Allow Operator Overrule (Yes/No) option specifies whether the operator is allowed to overrule the advice given by the IRIS system.
•Also on this page you can find the Result Lifetime in hours. This parameter describes how long the result of an IRIS scan remains valid. The previous scan results will become invalid if this time is exceeded, if the SMIF box is removed from the system or if IRIS or reticle handling is initialized.
--[p100]-- •On this same page the reject limits specify how many particles per particle bin and per inspection surface are acceptable. The Reject Limit Upper Surface (Lower Surface) specifies the maximum allowed number of particles per surface.
•A manual scan can be initiated via material handler. A reticle is scanned according to the conditions filled in on this material handling page. An automatic scan is automatically started depending on Factory Constants and batch control settings.
--[p100]-- The absolute minimum particle size is 10 µm (detection limit).
•The Operator Intervention option specifies how the operator is allowed to respond to an IRIS scan.
A= always, the operator must always decide whether to accept or reject the reticle.
R= reject only, the operator has to decide whether to use a reticle or not that has already been rejected by the IRIS system.
--[p100]-- •The inspection surfaces specify per layer of a job which surface of the reticle should be inspected for particles. In Factory Constants / Layer Data / Reticle Inspection / Inspection Surfaces the inspection surfaces are specified. U for upper surface (glass side) only, L for lower surface (pellicle side) only, B for both surfaces (glass and pellicle) and N for none if no inspection is needed.

===== [p141-142] Stage Movement Design > Solution for cogging: (5块) =====
--[p142]-- A Zeeman laser is a laser where a magnetic field is applied to the lasing gas. The presence of the magnetic field induces two wavelengths to be produced by the laser. The difference between both wavelengths is very small, its magnitude depends on the magnetic field strength. The magnetic field is kept constant.
A Zeeman laser is used mostly for stability purposes. For an interferometer with the precision of this one it is essential that the wavelength of the laser remains very constant.
--[p141]-- Delocalize the magnetic poles by placing the permanent magnets at a slight angle (Approx. 5°) with respect to the coils. (The coils are at the top of each picture.)
This way, a region around the permanent magnets is created, around which the long stroke motor force depends little on the magnitude of this distance.
The interferometer system by Hewlett Packard is used for stage position measurements. It is a relative system that monitors stage position changes.
--[p142]-- Stage movement induced pressure changes are in general too small to have an influence.
To exclude external conditions, the covers of the machine must be closed. The air showers around the stage then make sure that the air is properly conditioned.
--[p142]-- Having two frequencies has the advantage that it is possible to determine the direction in which the stage travels as well as correct for the occurrence of a Doppler frequency shift in the measurement beam (which is induced by stage movement).
The interferometer system is sensitive to environmental conditions. Pressure or temperature changes along the measurement beam will influence measured stage position. Stage movement induced pressure changes are in general too small to have an influence.
--[p142]-- It is a relative system that monitors stage position changes.
During alignment a ‘zero’ position is set. The interferometer software keeps track of the location of the stage with respect to this refence.
The accuracy in the PAS 5500 step&scan systems is such that a stage position shift of 0.6nm can be reliably measured. (Attained by a resolution of 512 counts per wavelength (633nm), and two reflections of the beam which results in the interferometer measuring double the movement of the stage.) Note that the slide demonstrates the principle only.

===== [p459-464] Effects of Lens Magnification (4块) =====
--[p459]-- scan direction
In this slide we se the same mag. error, but this time we have separated the resulting image error into 2 components:
Distortion and fading.
The distortion, which we have already discussed,is only in the X axis, but in this case, the fading is in the Y axis, because that is the error which changes most during the scan.
In this case, the distortion shows up as skew, but the fading is in the X axis.
Note that the static distortion differs from the rotation encountered before.
--[p464]--  MA affects overlay
The average position at which a specific point of the image is exposed, is determined by the Moving Average (MA) over the slit width of the instantaneous relative scanning error between the wafer stage and the reticle stage. This determines the real stage error and contributes to the error in overlay (this moving average corresponds to slow (low frequency, < 8 Hz) position errors of the stages and therefore produces overlay errors.
--[p460]-- Note that the static distortion differs from the rotation encountered before.
This is because here the cause of the rotation is assumed to be a RB rotation instead of an reticle/image rotation. On a step & scan system the effect would be the same. On a step & scan system we see a clear difference.
In this case, the error is caused by scan skew ( stage synchronization ), and the fading is purely in the X axis.
--[p461]-- It should be noted that finding the cause of a fading problem is often very difficult, because it can be caused by so many hardware problems ( reticle/wafer stage setup, scan synch., vibration, etc )
Dynamic IQ 48142 v00, 16/03/01 25
 MA affects overlay
During the exposure, each point on the die remains in the illumination slit for exactly the time required for correct exposure (by receiving the correct number of laser pulses)

===== [p213-213] Alignment > Alignment branches: (1块) =====
--[p213]-- The PAS5500 step & repeat system has a dual alignment system which means that the system uses two alignment branches, M1 and M2 originating from one laser. The system has one detector for each branch.

===== [p107-109] WAFER HANDLER (WH) > Wafer Pre-alignment System (WPS) (7块) =====
--[p107]-- The rotation unit, centering unit, edge sensor, mark sensor, dipod and discharge unit are all part of the WPS.
On the rotation unit, centering unit, dipod and discharge unit, wafers are detected by vacuum.
The centering unit can move in Y only.
The rotation unit can move in Z and Rz.
The dipod can move in Rz ( 60 -).
The discharge unit can move in Z.
The sequence of events (mark sensor not used):
– Rotation unit moves up to take the wafer from the end-effector robot
--[p107]-- The purpose of the Wafer Pre-alignment System (WPS) is to pre-align wafers (mechanically and/or optically) and load/unload wafers on the scanning wafer stage. The loading and unloading of wafers is done simultaneously by the two arms of the dipod (can be seen in the sequence of events below). Pre-alignment is the sequence that will position the wafer in such a way that the actual wafer alignment by the alignment system can be done accurately.
--[p107]-- – Dipod rotates +120-
– Dipod rotates -60- to go back to park position
– Dipod rotates -60-
– E-pins lower while dipod (arm 2) takes over the wafer
– Dipod rotates +120-
– Discharge unit goes up to take over the wafer from the dipod (arm 2)
– Dipod rotates -60- to go back to park position
– Discharge unit lowers to put the wafer on the end-effector robot
The edge sensor consists of a line CCD (charge coupled device) and illumination source.
--[p107]-- The sequence of events (mark sensor not used):
– Rotation unit rotates 360- while edge sensor detects the signal
– Rotation unit rotates the wafer in the correct orientation and lowers
– Centering unit takes over the wafer and moves in Y to correct for eccentricity
– Rotation unit goes up to take over the wafer from the centering unit
– Rotation unit rotates 360- to check the applied correction
--[p109]-- After this the rotation unit moves up again to take the wafer (vacuum exchanged).
Pre-alignment correction sequence:
– one $3 6 0 ^ { \circ }$ scan (this slide)
– rotate wafer such that all eccentricity is in the Y-direction (slide 10)
– Move centering unit to correct for eccentricity (slide 10)
– another $3 6 0 ^ { \circ }$ scan after the correction to check (slide 11)
– repeat sequence if necessary
--[p107]-- – Rotation unit goes up to take over the wafer from the centering unit
– Rotation unit rotates a few - to fine tune the position of the notch – Rotation unit rotates the wafer in such a way that the notch is positioned correctly (after loading by the dipod the wafer will be in the required position which is normally the back of the system)
– Dipod rotates -60-
– Rotation unit lowers, while dipod (arm 1) takes over the wafer
– Dipod rotates +120-
– E-pins (scanning wafer stage) move up to take over the wafer from the dipod (arm 1)
--[p109]-- The edge sensor consists of a line CCD (charge coupled device) and illumination source.
The CCD device generates a signal proportional to the number of segments that are illuminated. By scanning the wafer’s edge, the center and eccentricity of the wafer are calculated
The centering unit takes the wafer from the rotation unit when this one moves down. The wafer clamped by vacuum while the rotation unit releases the vacuum. Then the centering unit positions the center of the wafer above the center of the rotation unit by moving the wafer in Y.

===== [p289-289] REFLECTION IMAGE SENSOR (RIS) > Batch interval, Time interval and Interrupt batch (3块) =====
--[p289]-- In the machine constants you can choose when an Image Sensor scan will be done: at fixed time intervals, before each batch and whether or not a batch may be interrupted. In a job it is possible to override the last parameter (if it was enabled in the Machine Constants).
Automatic mode and measurement mode
In automatic mode the drift data (Machine Constants) are automatically updated, if they are within certain defined parameters. (See AP2)
--[p289]-- If you perform an Image Sensor scan you can choose if you want to update all these parameters ‘system corrections’ or if you only want to up-date parameters that have influence on the focus (image tilt and focus) ‘focus corrections’.
For both system and focus corrections you can choose automatic or measurement mode.
--[p289]-- If the Image Sensor operates in measure mode (set in Machine Constants) then the drift Machine Constants are only measured and not updated. They can be updated via a test. (Metrology Verification/Image Quality Control.)
Focus correction or system correction
The Image Sensor measures the following Image Quality parameters: Magnification, Rotation, Translation (X and Y), Image tilt (Rx and Ry) and Focus.

===== [p359-359] CONTAMINATION & TEMPERATURE CONTROL (C&T) > Exhaust Air System (1) (3块) =====
--[p359]-- • For cooling the laser module of the Advanced Alignment system (scanners), two small fans are used in this laser module that blow air into the ARMS air extraction pipe.
--[p359]-- The exhaust air system removes heat generated by various components of the machine. The exhaust air system covers electronics, lasers cooling and illumination cooling.
 Electronics & Laser cooling
• The exhaust air system removes heat generated by various components of the machine. The exhaust air system covers electronics, laser cooling, and illumination cooling.
• Electronic component temperature influences components life.
--[p359]-- • Electronic component temperature influences components life.
• A forced air cooling of the electronics is therefore applied by extracting air from the electronic racks. This air is blown into the fab environment or into a facility exhaust duct.
• An extra blower (Fan for scanners) located in the Air Control Cabinet (ACC) is needed to guarantee sufficient extraction in the cooling air flow process.
